\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where MCE refers to the Maurer-Cartan set of the respective dg shifted Lie algebra and Z refers to the cycles of the underlying chain complex.
40
40

40

As is well known, we can replace
Z
by homology if we replace MCE by its quotient by the gauge group action.

For
t=1
commutativity of the diagram is a standard fact about BD algebras, for general
t
it follows from equation (
4.1.16
).

Definition 4.1.17
.

Let
P
be an r-shifted Poisson algebra and
R
be an
r
-twisted BD algebra.
We call

p:R\rightarrow P

a
dequantization map
if it is a degree zero map of the underlying chain complexes and of shifted Lie algebras.

Remark 4.1.18
.

Note that we do not ask that this map respects the products, which is what we will see in our examples.

Lemma 4.1.19
.

Given two
r
-twisted BD algebras
A
,
B
the tensor product of their underlying graded vector spaces
A\otimes B
can be equipped with the structure of an
r
-twisted BD algebra in a natural way, extending the initial ones.

To define the shifted Poisson bracket on the tensor product we use both the respective algebra multiplications, denoted
m_{A}
and
m_{B}
, and the original shifted Poisson brackets. Precisely we have that

\displaystyle\{a_{1}\otimes b_{1},a_{2}\otimes b_{2}\}=(-1)^{|a_{2}|(|b_{1}|+r)}m_{A}(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle\otimes\{b_{1},b_{2}\}_{B}

(4.1.20)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{|b_{1}|(|a_{2}|+r)}\{a_{1},a_{2}\}_{A}\otimes m_{B}(b_{1},b_{2}).

Proof.

This is the same argument described in definition 2.7 and proposition-definition 2.2 of
[
72
]
, following
[
80
]
, section 5.8.1. Note however that we follow the convention of remark 2.3 (2) of
[
72
]
.
∎

We make some slightly ad-hoc definitions, meant as a first approach to a homotopy theory of BD algebras.

Definition 4.1.21
.

An
r-twisted dg-BD algebra
is a r-twisted BD algebra
A
, additionally equipped with a degree 1 differential
D:A\rightarrow A
, which is a derivation with respect to the product and commutes with respect of the BD differential.

Given such an r-twisted dg BD algebra
(A,D)
the homology
H_{*}(A,D)
is naturally a BD algebra.

Definition 4.1.22
.

We say that two r-twisted dg-BD algebra
(A,D_{1})
and
(B,D_{2})
are
quasi-isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism of BD algebra
H_{*}(A,D_{1})\rightarrow H_{*}(B,D_{2}).

Remark 4.1.23
.

There is a functor from r-twisted dg BD algebra to r-twisted BD algebra which is the identity on the underlying graded shifted Poisson algebra; the BD differential of the BD algebra is given by the sum of the initial BD differential of the dg-BD algebra plus the differential of the dg-BD algebra.

4.2
(Cyclic)
L_{\infty}
-Algebras and Lie Algebra Homology

We recall the following notions, basically citing the succinct presentation of
[
95
]
:

Let
(V,d)
be a chain complex. We denote the symmetric algebra by
SymV=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}V^{\otimes n}/I
, where
I
is the two-sided ideal generated by relations
x\otimes y-(-1)^{|x||y|}y\otimes x
. The product will be denoted by
\odot
. For example, the expressions
x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot x_{n}:=[x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n}]
generate
Sym^{n}V
as a vector space. Let
Sym^{+}V:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}Sym^{n}V
, with grading given by
|x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot x_{n}|=\sum_{j}|x_{j}|
. This space carries the structure of a graded cocommutative dg coalgebra without counit, with comultiplication given by

\Delta(x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot x_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\sqcup J=[n]\\
|I|,|J|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\epsilon(I,J)\bigodot_{i\in I}x_{i}\otimes\bigodot_{j\in J}x_{j}.

(4.2.1)

Here
\epsilon(I,J)
is the sign of the “shuffle” permutation bringing the elements of
I
and
J
corresponding to
odd

x
’s into increasing order. Note that
\epsilon(I,J)
implicitly depends on the degrees of the
x
’s. It is straightforward to check that the differential
d
, extended from the chain complex to the symmetric algebra via the Leibniz rule, is compatible with the comultiplication
\Delta
.

Definition 4.2.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\Delta,d)
,
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be dg coalgebras. A linear map
\mathcal{F}\in Hom_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
is called
degree
k
morphism of coalgebras
if
\Delta^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F})\circ\Delta
and it commutes with the underlying differentials. A linear map
Q\in Hom_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
is called a
degree
k
coderivation
on
\mathcal{C}
if
\Delta\circ Q=(Q\otimes 1+1\otimes Q)\circ\Delta
and it commutes with the underlying differential. Here we use the Koszul sign rule, e.g.,
(1\otimes Q)(x\otimes y)=(-1)^{k|x|}x\otimes Qy
etc.

Remark 4.2.3
.

Any coderivation
Q
on
Sym^{+}V
(coalgebra morphism
\mathcal{F}:Sym^{+}V\to Sym^{+}W
) is uniquely determined by its composition with the projection
Sym^{+}V\to Sym^{1}V=V
, respectively (
{Sym^{+}W\to Sym^{1}W=W}
). The restriction to
Sym^{n}V
of this composition will be denoted by
{Q_{n}\in Hom(Sym^{k}V,V)}
(
\mathcal{F}_{n}\in Hom(Sym^{k}V,W)
) and called the
n
-th “Taylor coefficient” of
Q
(
\mathcal{F}
).

Definition 4.2.4
.

An
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on a chain complex
(L,d)
is a degree 1 coderivation
l
on
Sym^{+}(L[1])
such that
l^{2}=0
and that
[l,d]=0
. A morphism of
L_{\infty}
algebras

{F}:(L,l)\rightsquigarrow(L^{\prime},l^{\prime})

is a degree 0 coalgebra morphism
C({F}):S^{+}(L[1])\to Sym^{+}((L^{\prime})[1])
such that
C({F})\circ l=l^{\prime}\circ C({F})
and that commutes with the underlying differentials.

In components, the
L_{\infty}
-relations read

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\sqcup J=[n]\\
|I|,|J|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\epsilon(I,J)l_{|J|+1}(l_{|I|}(\bigodot_{i\in I}x_{i})\odot\bigodot_{j\in J}x_{j})=0.

Remark 4.2.5
.

In fact we assume that
l_{1}=0
since we can just absorb it into the differential
d
of the underlying chain complex.

Example 4.2.6
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},d,\{\_,\_\})
be a differential graded Lie algebra. Then the assignments
l_{1}(x)=dx
,
l_{2}(x,y)=(-1)^{|x|}\{x,y\}
,
Q_{n}=0
for
n=3,4,..
define an
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}
.
Here and everywhere in this thesis
|x|
is the degree wrt. the grading on the coalgebra.

Example 4.2.7
.

An
L_{\infty}
-morphism
\mathcal{F}
between dglas
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
has to satisfy the relations

l_{1}^{\prime}\mathcal{F}_{n}(x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot x_{n})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\sqcup J=[n]\\
|I|,|J|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\epsilon(I,J)l_{2}^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}_{|I|}(\bigodot_{i\in I}x_{i})\odot\mathcal{F}_{|J|}(\bigodot_{j\in J}x_{j}))=\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon(i,1,\dots,\hat{i},\dots,n)\mathcal{F}_{n}(l_{1}(x_{i})\odot x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\hat{x}_{i}\odot\cdots\odot x_{n})+\\
+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\epsilon(i,j,\dots,\hat{i},\dots,\hat{j},\dots,n)\mathcal{F}_{n-1}(l_{2}(x_{i}\odot x_{j})\odot x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\hat{x}_{i}\odot\cdots\odot\hat{x}_{j}\odot\cdots x_{n}).

(4.2.8)

Here the factor
\epsilon(i,j,1,..,\hat{i},..,\hat{j},..,n)
is the sign of the permutation on the
odd

x
’s that brings
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
to the left and analogously for
\epsilon(i,1,\dots,\hat{i},\dots,n)
.

Definition 4.2.9
.

Given an
L_{\infty}
-algebra

L

we call the resulting chain complex

C_{*}^{+}(L):=\big(Sym_{+}(L[1]),d+l\big)

the (reduced) Chevalley-Eilenberg chains of
L
.
We further adopt the notation
{C_{*}(L):=\big(Sym(L[1]),d+l\big)},
the (non-reduced) Chevalley-Eilenberg chains.

We define

C^{*}(L):=Hom(C_{*}(L),k),

the Chevalley-Eilenberg cochains of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L
and denote
C^{*}_{+}(L):=Hom(C_{*}^{+}(L),k).

Recall from remark
4.2.3
that an
L_{\infty}
-morphism
can be entangled in saying that there are maps

\phi_{n}:L_{1}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow L_{2}

of certain degree and satisfying relations.
For instance
\phi_{1}
is just a chain map.

Definition 4.2.10
.

An
L_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism
is an
L_{\infty}
-morphism such that
\phi_{1}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

We say that an
L_{\infty}
-morphism is strict if
\phi_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 2
.

Definition 4.2.11
.

A
cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
of dimension
d
is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L
together with a symmetric non-degenerate pairing

\langle\_,\_\rangle:L\otimes L\rightarrow k[-d]

such that
S:=\langle d+l\_,\_\rangle\in C^{*}(L),
i.e. such that it is symmetrically invariant.
Note that in this case
S
is an element of degree
3-d
. We denote
I:=\langle l\_,\_\rangle.

We limit ourselves to strict cyclic
L_{\infty}
-morphisms:

Definition 4.2.12
.

A
cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-morphism
is a strict
L_{\infty}
-morphism such that

\langle F_{1}(x),F_{1}(y)\rangle=\langle x,y\rangle.

Remark 4.2.13
.

Note that a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-morphism is automatically injective.

Remark 4.2.14
.

One should be able to give a more abstract definition of a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra and
L_{\infty}
-morphisms, compare the
A_{\infty}
-case later.
See for instance section 5.2 of
[
56
]
, in the context of
L_{\infty}
-spaces.

4.3
A_{\infty}
-Categories and Cyclic Homology

An
A_{\infty}
-category is a homotopically better behaved generalization of the notion of a dg-category, further relevant since Fukaya categories of symplectic manifolds are
not
dg categories, but do carry an
A_{\infty}
-category structure:

Let
B
be a set and
V_{B}:=\{V_{ij}\}_{i,j\in B}
be a set of chain complexes.
Then its bar-construction
41
41

41

In the sense that it is the Bar complex of a dg-category with zero compositions.
is defined by

BarV_{B}:=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigoplus_{\lambda_{0},\cdots,\lambda_{n}\in B}V_{\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}}[1]\otimes V_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{\lambda_{n-1},\lambda_{n}}[1]

(4.3.1)

and it is naturally a dg coalgebra
(BarV_{B},d)
whose comultiplication, see e.g.
[
66
]
, example 2.1.6, we don’t give an extra symbol.

Definition 4.3.2
.

A
small
A_{\infty}
-category

\mathcal{C}
is given by a set of objects
Ob(\mathcal{C})
, a set of chain complexes
\{V_{\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}}\}_{\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}\in Ob(\mathcal{C})}
and a collection of maps

m_{n}\colon V_{\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}}[1]\otimes V_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}[1]\otimes\ldots\otimes V_{\lambda_{n-1},\lambda_{n}}[1]\to V_{\lambda_{0},\lambda_{n}}[1]

for all
n\geq 1
and all tuples of objects
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n}).

We can uniquely extend those to a coderivation

m_{\mathcal{C}}:BarV_{Ob(\mathcal{C})}\rightarrow BarV_{Ob(\mathcal{C})}

and we require that

m_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}=0

and that it commutes with the internal differential
d
of the chain complex
(BarV_{Ob(\mathcal{C})},d)
.

In this case we denote
V_{\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}}=:Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})
and

(BarV_{Ob(\mathcal{C})},d+m_{\mathcal{C}})=:(Bar\mathcal{C},d+m_{\mathcal{C}}).

Remark 4.3.3
.

We will only consider small
A_{\infty}
-categories in this paper and thus drop this adjective from now on.

Definition 4.3.4
.

We say that an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
is
unital
if there is an element

1_{i}\in Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i})
for all
\lambda_{i}\in Ob(\mathcal{C})
such that

m_{2}(g,1_{i})=g
for all
g\in Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda_{k},\lambda_{i})
,

\lambda_{k}\in Ob(\mathcal{C})
and
m_{2}(1_{i},f)=f
for all
f\in Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{l})
,
\lambda_{l}\in Ob(\mathcal{C})

and if in
m_{n}(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots)
any entry is one of the
1_{i}
then
m_{n}(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots)=0
for
n\geq 3
.

Remark 4.3.5
.

We can somewhat unpack the definition of an
A_{\infty}
-category:

•

The fact that
m_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}=0
is equivalent to the infinite list of relations, one for each
n\geq 1
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,t\geq 0,s\geq 1\\
r+s+t=n\end{subarray}}m_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes m_{s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})=0.

(4.3.6)

Here
id
denotes ambiguously the identity on whichever Hom-space it is applied to.

•

Using this it is easy to see that in the case that
m_{\mathcal{C}}
is only non-zero on
Bar^{2}V_{B}
,
i.e. on the summand
n=2
, we recover the definition of a (not necessarily unital) differential graded category; where
f\circ g=m_{2}(g,f)
for composable
f,g.

•

In fact we assume that
m_{1}=0
for the rest of this article since we can just absorb it into the differential of the underlying chain complexes.

Definition 4.3.7
.

A
functor between two
A_{\infty}
-categories

F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is given by a map of sets

F:Ob(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow Ob(\mathcal{D})

and maps

F_{n}:Hom_{\mathcal{C}}({\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}})[1]\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{C}}({\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{C}}({\lambda_{n-1},\lambda_{n}})[1]\rightarrow Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(\lambda_{0}),F(\lambda_{n}))[1],

for all
n\geq 1
and all tuples of objects
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n}).

We can uniquely extend this to a map of coalgebras, which we require to determine a chain map

F_{*}:\ (Bar(\mathcal{C}),d+m_{\mathcal{C}})\rightarrow(Bar\mathcal{D}),d+m_{\mathcal{D}}).

We denote by
A_{\infty}cat
the category whose objects are
A_{\infty}
-categories and with morphisms given by
A_{\infty}
-functors.

Remark 4.3.8
.

Again we can unpack the requirement that
F_{*}
intertwines the differentials.
Equivalently it means that

\sum_{r+s+t=n}F_{r+1+t}(id^{\otimes r}\otimes m_{\mathcal{C},s}\otimes id^{\otimes t})=\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{l}=n}m_{\mathcal{D},l}(F_{i_{1}}\otimes F_{i_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes F_{i_{l}})

(4.3.9)

for
n\geq 1
and where
m_{1}=d
.
Notably
F_{1}
induces maps of chain complexes on the Hom spaces.

Definition 4.3.10
.

We say that an
A_{\infty}
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
of unital
A_{\infty}
-categories is
unital
if
F_{1}(1_{i})=1_{F(i)}
and
F_{n}(\cdots,1_{i},\cdots)=0
for all
n\geq 2
.

Definition 4.3.11
.

We call an
A_{\infty}
-functor

F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}

strict
if
F_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 2
.

Given an unital
A_{\infty}
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
taking homology induces a functor of ordinary categories
H_{*}F:H_{*}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow H_{*}\mathcal{D}.

Definition 4.3.12
.

We call an unital
A_{\infty}
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
an
equivalence
if the induced functor

H_{*}F_{1}:H_{*}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow H_{*}\mathcal{D}

of ordinary categories is an equivalence.

Let us recall some further notions some of which we, strictly speaking, won’t need; thus we are relatively fast in doing so:

Given an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
there is dg-category of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules as described in
[
51
]
, section 3.1 and references therein.
Further in loc. cit. it is explained that there are certain objects called the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
, respectively the linear dual diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}^{\vee}
in this category.
Then one can define

Definition 4.3.13
.

CH_{*}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{C}^{e}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\in Ch,

given by the derived tensor product in the category of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules (see e.g.
[
51
]
, section 3.4).
Its homology is called
Hochschild homology
.

Remark 4.3.14
.

There are explicit complexes that compute this derived tensor product, which is one way to see that there are maps of chain complexes, for all objects
x
of
\mathcal{C}

\iota_{x}:Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x,x)\rightarrow CH_{*}(\mathcal{C}).

Let us come back to the notion of Hochschild homology.
One of its important properties is that there is an
S^{1}
-action on Hochschild chains (
[
27
]
, in our context eg.
[
52
]
, section 3.2).
By taking its homotopy
S^{1}
orbits we arrive at

Definition 4.3.15
.

Given an
A_{\infty}
-category we call the homology of

CC_{*}(\mathcal{C}):=CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})_{hS^{1}}\in Ch

its
cyclic homology
. A concrete model is given as follows: Denote by
u
a formal variable of degree
-2
. There is a chain map of degree 1, called Connes operator,

B:CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow CH_{*}(\mathcal{C}),

on the graded vector space
CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})
on which the differential
d_{Hoch}
computes Hochschild homology; it encodes the
S^{1}
-action.
B
commutes with the Hochschild differential. The following chain complex computes the homotopy
S^{1}
orbits, that is cyclic homology:

CC_{*}(\mathcal{C})\simeq\big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}],d_{Hoch}+uB\big).

Remark 4.3.16
.

Thus just by abstract reasons we get a map
\pi:CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow CC_{*}(\mathcal{C}).

Finally we want to consider a smaller model computing the cyclic (co)homology of an
A_{\infty}
-category:

Construction 4.3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category and consider the map given by cyclic permutation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})[1]\otimes Hom(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes Hom(\lambda_{n},\lambda_{0})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ni}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{01}\otimes a_{12}\otimes a_{23}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{no}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})[1]\otimes Hom(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})[1]\otimes\cdots\otimes Hom(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ni}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{*}a_{12}\otimes a_{23}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{no}\otimes a_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

Here
(-1)^{*}
comes from the Koszul sign rule.
We extend
t
to
Bar\mathcal{C}
by zero on the subspaces that are not of the above form, i,e, where
\lambda_{n}\neq\lambda_{0}
and denote it by the same letter.

Lemma 4.3.18
.

Given an
A_{\infty}
-category the differential
m_{\mathcal{C}}+d
on
Bar\mathcal{C}
restricts to
{ker(1-t)}
.

Proof.

This is a standard result, which follows in the same way as in lemma 2.1.1 of
[
76
]
.
∎

Finally we have to include a degree shift by 1:

Theorem 4.3.19

(‘Cyclic Words’)
.

The cyclic chain complex computes cyclic homology

(Cyc_{*}^{+}(\mathcal{C}),m_{\mathcal{C}}+d):=\big(ker(1-t)[-1],m_{\mathcal{C}}+d\big)\simeq CC_{*}(\mathcal{C})

Proof.

This is well known.
For the algebra case see
[
76
]
, theorem 2.1.5, in our generality eg. around definition 2.5 and remark 2.6 of
[
3
]
.
∎

Definition 4.3.20
.

The cochain complex

(Cyc^{*}_{+}(\mathcal{C}),m_{\mathcal{C}}+d):=Hom(Cyc_{*}^{+}(\mathcal{C}),k)

with the induced differential is called
cyclic cochain complex
and its homology is referred to as the
cyclic cohomology
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Remark 4.3.21
.

We think of a cyclic cochain as a cyclic word in the dual of the Hom-spaces with matching ‘boundary condition’.
We adopt the notation

(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})\in Cyc^{*}_{+}(\mathcal{C})[-1],

where by definition
a_{i}\in Hom(\lambda,\mu)^{\vee}[-1]
and
a_{i+1}\in Hom(\mu,\nu)^{\vee}[-1]
for
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in Ob(\mathcal{C})
.

Lastly we introduce important properties of
A_{\infty}
-categories:

Definition 4.3.22
.

We say that an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
is
smooth
if
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
is a compact, ie. a perfect object in the dg category of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules.

We say that an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
is
proper
if
Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)\in Ch
is perfect for all
x,y\in Ob(\mathcal{C}).

Remark 4.3.23
.

Compare also
[
12
]
ex. 2.8 and ex. 2.9 for a more abstract phrasing, in the setting of dg categories, which shows that the notions smooth and proper are complementary in a certain sense.

4.4
Calabi-Yau
A_{\infty}
-Categories

For the purposes of this thesis we consider
A_{\infty}
-categories with an extra structure, which most generally may be phrased as requiring a (smooth and) proper Calabi-Yau structure. We work with a convenient strictification of such a structure, called cyclic and leave it to further investigations how to obtain more homotopically coherent results.
Compare
[
24
]

[
13
]
for relevant results in this direction and
[
3
]
for an answer to such investigations in the purely ‘closed’ setting.

In any case, a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category is canonically a strong proper Calabi-Yau category and thus a weak one, see around corollary 2.26 of
[
3
]
. A weak proper Calabi-Yau category together with a splitting of the non-commutative Hodge filtration (a central ingredient in this paper, which we introduce later in definition
4.5.1
) naturally induce a strong proper Calabi-Yau structure, see lemma 3.16 of
[
3
]
. Lastly in characteristic zero one can strictify a strong proper Calabi-Yau category to a cyclic one, see
[
66
]
theorem 10.7.
Thus we may pretend that all these notions are roughly equivalent for our purposes.
42
42

42

However, the notion of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category is not preserved under general
A_{\infty}
-functors (whereas the one of proper Calabi-Yau categories is).

Definition 4.4.1
.

Given
\mathcal{C}
a proper
A_{\infty}
-category we
call a choice of quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules

\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}^{\vee}[-d]

a
weak proper Calabi-Yau
structure of dimension
d
.

Remark 4.4.2

(See rmk. 49 of
[
52
]
)
.

A weak proper Calabi-Yau structure of dimension
d
, also called weak right Calabi-Yau structure, may be equivalently described by saying that there is a map of complexes

tr:CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow k[-d]

such that the induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(y,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[-d]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{2}_{x,y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tr}

is non-degenerate on homology, for all objects
x,y\in Ob\mathcal{C}
.
If we additionally ask that the trace map comes from a map from cyclic homology along the map
\pi
from remark
4.3.16
, this is called a strong proper Calabi-Yau structure.

Remark 4.4.3
.

There is (are) also the notion(s) of a (weak and strong) smooth Calabi-Yau, also called (weak and strong) left Calabi-Yau structure on a smooth
A_{\infty}
-category.
See definitions 12 and 13 of
[
52
]
. In fact if a category is both smooth and proper, the notions of smooth and proper Calabi-Yau structures coincide; see proposition 6.10 of
[
50
]
.

Definition 4.4.4
.

A
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
of degree
d
, denoted
(\mathcal{C},\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{C}})
, is an

A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
together with a symmetric non-degenerate chain map

\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mu\lambda}:\ Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\mu,\lambda)\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda,\mu)\rightarrow k[-d]

for all
\mu,\lambda\in Ob(\mathcal{C})
such that

S_{\mathcal{C}}:=\langle(m+d)\_,\_\rangle\in Cyc^{*}_{+}(\mathcal{C})[-1],

(4.4.5)

i.e. is cyclically symmetric.

Note that in this case
S_{\mathcal{C}}
is of degree
3-d
.
We further denote by

I_{\mathcal{C}}:=\langle m\_,\_\rangle\in Cyc^{*}_{+}(\mathcal{C})[-1],

which has the same degree.

Remark 4.4.6
.

See remark 9 of
[
52
]
for the fact that any cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category is canonically a strong (and thus weak) proper Calabi-Yau.
Further it is explained that in characteristic zero one can strictify a strong proper Calabi-Yau category to a cyclic one.
However, the notion of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category is not preserved under general
A_{\infty}
-functors, whereas the one of proper Calabi-Yau categories is.

Without giving details we state the result from definition 2.15 of
[
3
]
that there is a functor

\Omega^{2,cl}:(A_{\infty}Cat)^{op}\rightarrow Ch,

which associates to an
A_{\infty}
-category a chain complex called its non-commutative 2-forms.
As described in definition 2.23 of loc. cit. a cyclic structure
\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{C}}
on an

A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
induces an element
\rho_{\mathcal{C}}\in\Omega^{2,cl}(\mathcal{C}).

Definition 4.4.7
.

Given two cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories
(\mathcal{C},\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{C}})
and
(\mathcal{D},\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{D}})

a
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-functor
is an
A_{\infty}
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
such that

F^{*}\rho_{\mathcal{D}}=\rho_{\mathcal{C}},

where
\rho_{\mathcal{D}}
respectively
\rho_{\mathcal{C}}
refers to the non-commutative 2-form associated to the cyclic structure by the previous remark.

Remark 4.4.8
.

This definition should reduce to definition 5.3 (2) of
[
57
]
in the case of a category with one object.
Note that the authors use the term symplectic where we used the term cyclic.
See equation 13 of
[
3
]
for an explicit equivalent description of definition
4.4.7
.
In particular one has

\langle F_{1}(x),F_{1}(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}=\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathcal{C}}.

(4.4.9)

Denote by
(cyc_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}
the (ordinary) category whose objects are cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories of degree
d
and whose morphisms are cyclic strict
A_{\infty}
-functors.

Definition 4.4.10
.

We say

V_{B}:=(V_{ij},\langle\_,\_\rangle_{ij})_{i,j\in B}

is a
collection of cyclic chain complexes
if it is a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
with
m_{\mathcal{C}}=0
and
B=Ob(\mathcal{C})
.

4.5
Splitting of non-commutative Hodge filtration

Definition 4.5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category. A
splitting of the non-commutative Hodge filtration
is a chain map

s:(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C}),d_{Hoch})\rightarrow(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\llbracket u\rrbracket,d_{Hoch}+uB)

splitting the canonical projection

\pi:(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\llbracket u\rrbracket),d_{Hoch}+uB)\rightarrow(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C}),d_{Hoch}),

that is we require
s\circ\pi=id
.

Remark 4.5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded smooth and proper
A_{\infty}
-category. By
[
65
]
the non-commutative Hodge to de Rham spectral sequence degenerates and converges to negative cyclic homology, as explained eg. in the proof of theorem 5.45 of
[
86
]
. Because we are working over a field of characteristic zero this implies that there is a (non-canonical) identification of negative cyclic homology

HC_{*}^{-}(\mathcal{C})\cong HH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\llbracket u\rrbracket.

(4.5.3)

A splitting of the non-commutative Hodge filtration provides in particular the choice of such an isomorphism. Indeed, extending a splitting
s

u
-linearly gives a map

(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\llbracket u\rrbracket,d_{Hoch})\rightarrow(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\llbracket u\rrbracket,d_{Hoch}+uB),

which we can think of as a formal power series of endomorphisms of Hochschild chains whose first term is invertible (in fact it is the identity, by the splitting condition), thus the whole formal power series is invertible. Thus this map induces an isomorphism (
4.5.3
).

5
Closed String Field Theory

In this section we introduce the classical and quantum algebras of observables of closed SFT. We first recall the quantum algebra of observables of
free
closed SFT
[
38
,
19
]
, which we used already in the introduction. Then we define the classical algebras of observables of interacting closed SFT, which is novel in this thesis. We recall BCOV theory
[
31
]
and present some comparisons to the previous algebraic theory of SFT, for the classical interacting part in the form of a conjecture.

For completeness we also mention the much later chapter
13.3
on backreacted closed string field theory.

5.1
Free Quantum Closed String Field Theory from CY Categories

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category of degree
d
. Then we call
free quantum observables of the closed string field theory associated to
\mathcal{C}
the
(2d-5)
-twisted Beilinson-Drinfeld over
k\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket
(recalling definition
4.1.3
)

\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C}):=\Big(Sym\Big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}][d-2]\Big)^{-*}\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket,d_{Hoch}+uB+\gamma\Delta_{c},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big).

In detail, the map
d_{Hoch}+uB+\gamma\Delta_{c}
is a differential and
\{\_,\_\}_{c}
is a
(2d-5)
shifted Poisson bracket, which together with the natural multiplication satisfy the BD relation
4.1.4
. More precisely the algebraic operations are defined exactly as in
[
3
]
, that is
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathfrak{h}_{\mathcal{C}}
, (see
[
3
]
, page 35 for the definition of the latter) just that we carry the shifts with us and we further ignore the variable
\lambda
from there. Further the notation
-*
means that we reverse the grading of what is inside the brackets, which is why eg.
|d_{Hoch}|=1,
here, despite the homological convention. See also section 4.1 of
[
19
]
for a more detailed description of the algebraic operations in the case of a category with one object.

Remark 5.1.2
.

This algebraic structure was originally described in
[
38
]
, but with slightly different grading conventions. Here we chose to follow
[
19
]
, but had to perform yet different shifts to be compatible with the open sector (that is notably for section
12
). We should remark that theorem
1.2.7
is formulated using different shifts. There one uses

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}:=Sym\Big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}][d]\Big)\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket[1-2d],

see
[
19
]
for the algebraic structures and especially appendix A for the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading. One may try to formulate theorem
1.2.7
also with the conventions for
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})
, but we haven’t done this, yet. Because of this we also need to formulate definition
5.2.2
below with respect to the grading set-up for
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}
.

Definition 5.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category of degree
d
. We denote by
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{triv}
the
(2d-5)
-twisted BD algebra which has the same underlying graded algebra as
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C}),
but with zero bracket and whose differential is just induced from the derivation
d_{Hoch}+uB
.

Definition 5.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category of degree
d
. We denote by
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}
the
(2d-5)
-twisted BD algebra which has the same underlying graded algebra as
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C}),
but with zero bracket and whose differential is just induced from the derivation
d_{Hoch}
.

5.2
Classical Interacting Closed String Field Theory from CY Categories

Denote by
S^{c}_{g=0}
the genus zero part of the closed string vertices, recalling definition (
1.2.5
) and equation (
1.2.9
). Let
\mathcal{C}
be a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category. Then it follows from the genus zero part of equation (
1.2.9
) and theorem
1.2.7
that

(d_{hoch}+uB+\{\rho(S^{c}_{g=0}),\_\}_{c})^{2}=0,

ie. it defines a differential on the shifted Poisson algebra
\Big(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big).

Recalling that

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0}=Sym\Big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}][d]\Big)

the differential equivalently defines a co
L_{\infty}
-algebra on the graded vector space
{CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}][d+1]}
, which induces by dualizing an
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on

\Big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}][(d+1)]^{\vee},d_{hoch}+uB\Big),

(5.2.1)

a chain complex computing cyclic cohomology of
\mathcal{C}
(shifted by
-d-1
). We denote this
L_{\infty}
-algebra by
L(\mathcal{C}).

Definition 5.2.2
.

Given a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}

we call
classical interacting closed string field theory
associated to
\mathcal{C}
the datum of the dg shifted Poisson algebra

\Big(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0},\cdot,d_{hoch}+uB+\{\rho(S^{c}_{g=0}),\_\}_{c},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big).

and
L(\mathcal{C})
, the
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
5.2.1
), the prior being its observables and the latter describing its space of fields.

Remark 5.2.3
.

The shifted Poisson structure
\{\_,\_\}_{c}
is not
not
induced from a non-degenerate odd pairing on the space of fields (
5.2.1
), which is what usually happens in the BV approach to (quantum) field theories. See section 1.4 of
[
33
]
and
[
15
]
for a general discussion of
degenerate shifted Poisson structures.

5.3
BCOV Theory

In
[
31
]
and
[
33
]
Costello-Li introduce BCOV theory, which is a version of Kodaira-Spencer gravity when formulated in the BV formalism. We recall in proposition
5.3.11
how BCOV theory is related to the objects from the previous section
5.1
; see also section 4 of
[
31
]
.

5.3.1
Quantum Free BCOV Theory

Let
X
be a smooth compact Calabi-Yau manifold. The central object is the following chain complex built from polyvector fields

\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X):=(PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)\llbracket u\rrbracket[2],\bar{\partial}+u\partial).

(5.3.1)

The abelian
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)[-1]
describes the fields of (perturbative) free BCOV theory. We want to equip

\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big):=\widehat{Sym}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)^{\vee}\big)\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket,

the space of completed symmetric powers of (strong) linear functionals
43
43

43

Here we use the grading convention that for
V
a graded vector space
(V^{\vee})_{n}=(V_{-n})^{\vee}
.
on the chain complex (
5.3.1
), with the structure of a BD algebra. Because of the infinite dimensional nature of the objects appearing one has to use regularization techniques, which we do following the theory developed in
[
28
]
. For that we fix a Kähler metric
g
on
X
. Then we can define for every
L>0
a square zero map

\Delta_{L}:\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big),

which is roughly defined by contracting with an element
K_{L}^{g}\in PV(X)\otimes PV(X)
and applying the map
\partial
on powers of
u=0
, zero otherwise; see section 3.1 of
[
31
]
for details. By imposing the BD relation (
4.1.4
)
\Delta_{L}
determines a shifted Poisson bracket
\{\_,\_\}_{L}
. Thus we can formulate:

Definition 5.3.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth compact Calabi-Yau manifold (
L>0
and
g
a Kähler metric). Then the BD algebra

\Big(\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big),\bar{\partial}+u\partial+\gamma\Delta_{L},\{\_,\_\}_{L}\Big)

(5.3.3)

are
the (regularized) quantum observables of free BCOV theory
.

Remark 5.3.4
.

Definition
5.3.2
is independent from the choice of a Kähler metric up to homotopy. See the discussion around theorem 3.6.2 of
[
31
]
for a precise statement.

Remark 5.3.5
.

There is a natural pairing on polyvector fields, given by

\langle\mu,\lambda\rangle=\int_{X}Tr(\mu\wedge\lambda),

(5.3.6)

for
\mu,\lambda\in PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)[2]
and where for
\alpha\in PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)[2]

Tr(\alpha)=(\omega\vee\alpha)\wedge\omega

with
\omega
the holomorphic volume form of
X
, see section 2.2 of
[
31
]
. For
L=0
the element
K_{L}^{g}
is given by the inverse of the non-degenerate pairing (
5.3.6
), but it is of distributional nature (see section 4.3 of
[
31
]
). The elements
K_{l}^{g}
are homologous to it.

Remark 5.3.7
.

As in remark
5.2.3
the shifted Poisson bracket
\{\_,\_\}_{L}
is not induced from a (smooth functions of) non-degenerate pairing(s) on the (fibers over X of the) space of fields
\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)
. If we however just pretend that this is the case (and take
L=0
, see the previous remark
5.3.5
) and denote by the
\langle\_,\_\rangle_{im}
the induced pairing on fields from this imagined inverse we recover the original kinetic term of the BCOV theory from
[
10
]

\int_{X}Tr(\mu\wedge\bar{\partial}\partial^{-1}\mu)=\langle(\bar{\partial}+u\partial)\mu,\mu\rangle_{im}

(5.3.8)

where
\mu\in PV^{1,1}(X)[2].

We recall the notions of dg-BD algebras and of quasi-isomorphism of dg-BD algebras from definition
4.1.21
and
4.1.22
. The dg-BD algebra (note the extra comma)

\Big(\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big),\bar{\partial}+u\partial,\gamma\Delta_{L},\{\_,\_\}_{L}\Big)

(5.3.9)

and for
\mathcal{C}
a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category the dg-BD algebra

\Big(Sym\Big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}][d-2]\Big)^{-*}\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket,d_{Hoch}+uB,\gamma\Delta_{c},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big)

(5.3.10)

induce the BD algebras (
5.3.3
) respectively
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})
under the functor from dg-BD algebras to BD algebras from remark
4.1.23
.

Proposition 5.3.11
.

Let
X
be a smooth compact Calabi-Yau manifold, so that its category of bounded coherent sheaves
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}_{dg}(X)
is a smooth proper Calabi-Yau category. The dg-BD algebra from (
5.3.9
) is quasi-isomorphic to the dg BD algebra from (
5.3.10
) for
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}_{dg}(X)
.
44
44

44

Or rather a cyclic model of the latter via theorem 2.37 of
[
3
]
, which we refer to for the independence of this choice.

Sketch of Proof.

This follows because we can identify the respective dg-BD algebras as Fock spaces associated to symplectic chain complexes, see section 7 of
[
38
]
respectively section 4 of
[
31
]
.
45
45

45

In fact the two cited sources differ by the fact that in
[

]
the symplectic vector space is used, whereas in
[
31
]
the dual symplectic vector space is used. In the identification (
5.3.12
) we are implicitly reversing this, again. See also remark
5.3.5
.

Those symplectic chain complexes are quasi-isomorphic, thus implying the claim: The underlying chain complexes of these symplectic chain complexes are given by the left and right most object of diagram (
5.3.12
) below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-19.91684pt\Big(CH_{*}(D^{b}_{dg}(X))((u))[d-2],d_{H}+uB\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big(H^{*}(X)((u))[d-2],0\Big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Big(PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)((u))[2],\bar{\partial}-u\partial\Big).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(5.3.12)

The first map is the HKR map (see
[
70
]
), the second map is described in proposition 5.2.1 of
[
31
]
. The compatible symplectic structures of the symplectic chain complexes are given by following diagram (commuting on homology with respect to the differentials in diagram
5.3.12
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CH_{*}(D^{b}_{dg}(X))((u))[d-2]^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[2d-6]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(X)((u))[d-2]^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[2d-6]}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[2d-6],}

(5.3.13)

where the upper most horizontal map is given by the higher residue pairing induced from the Mukai pairing (see section 7.1 of
