∎

5.3.2
Classical Interacting BCOV Theory

The original interaction term of classical BCOV theory from
[
10
]
was defined as

I_{cubic}:=\int_{X}Tr(\mu^{3}),

(5.3.14)

recalling the pairing from equation (
5.3.6
) and for
\mu\in PV^{1,1}(X)[2]
(that is for the ordinary, not (anti) ghost/field content and not taking into account the gravitational descendants encoded by the
u
parameter).
However, it is not clear how to extend the functional (
5.3.14
) to positive powers of
u
in
(PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)\llbracket u\rrbracket[2]
.
On the other hand the functional (
5.3.14
) induces a Lie algebra structure on
PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)[1]
via the square zero vector field

\{I_{cubic},\_\}_{L=0}:\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0}

which is well-defined for
L=0
, see section 5.9.4 of
[
28
]
).
We can see that the resulting Lie structure on
PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)[1]
exactly comes from the standard Schouten bracket
[\_\_]_{S}
on polyvector fields, see eg. section 2.1 of
[
31
]
.
We can extend the Schouten bracket
u
-linearly to
(PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)\llbracket u\rrbracket[1]
. This leads Costello-Li to the following definition.

Definition 5.3.15
.

Classical interacting BCOV theory formulated in the BV formalism
is the datum of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra

\Big(PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)\llbracket u\rrbracket[1],\bar{\partial}+u\partial,[\_\_]_{S}\Big)

(5.3.16)

and the shifted Poisson algebra

\Big(\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0},\bar{\partial}+u\partial+d_{CE},\{\_,\_\}_{L=0}\Big),

(5.3.17)

where
d_{CE}
is the differential induced from the
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure (
5.3.16
).

Costello-Li provide an alternative description of definition
5.3.15
. They note that there is an element

I_{BCOV}\in\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0}

(5.3.18)

which satisfies the classical master equation

(\bar{\partial}+u\partial)I_{BCOV}+\frac{1}{2}\{I_{BCOV},I_{BCOV}\}_{L=0}=0

or in other words it gives a square zero vector field

\Big(\bar{\partial}+u\partial+\{I_{BCOV},\_\}_{L=0}\Big)^{2}=0,

which induces an
L_{\infty}
-structure on the complex underlying (
5.3.16
).
Costello-Li further prove that there is an
L_{\infty}
-quasi isomorphism between the
L_{\infty}
-algebra determined by the square zero vector field
\bar{\partial}+u\partial+\{I_{BCOV},\_\}_{L=0}
and the
L_{\infty}
-algebra (
5.3.16
), see section 6 of
[
31
]
, also around section 8 of
[
33
]
.

We expect the structures discussed in this subsection to be related to the structures from subsection
5.2
, which we make precise via following conjecture.

Conjecture 5.3.19
.

The dg-Lie algebra (
5.3.16
) is
L_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphic to the
L_{\infty}
-algebra of classical interacting closed SFT from definition
5.2.2
applied to the smooth proper Calabi-Yau category
{\mathcal{C}=D^{b}_{dg}(X)}
of quasi-coherent sheafs.
46
46

46

Or better a cyclic model of the latter via theorem 2.37 of
[
3
]
.

Let us make some remarks supporting this conjecture. We can identify the underlying chain complexes of the
L_{\infty}
-algebras featuring in conjecture
5.3.19
via

\Big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}][(d+1)]^{\vee},d_{hoch}+uB\Big)\simeq\Big(CH^{*}(\mathcal{C})\llbracket u\rrbracket[(d+1)],d_{hoch}+uB\Big)\simeq\Big(PV^{\bullet,\bullet}(X)\llbracket u\rrbracket[1],\bar{\partial}+u\partial\Big),

(5.3.20)

where we first use the higher residue pairing and then the HKR isomorphism (see eg. section 3.3 of
[
33
]
). Next we make some remarks about how also the higher brackets of the
L_{\infty}
-algebras featuring in conjecture
5.3.19
should be related:
We recall that the
L_{\infty}
-structure (
5.3.16
) is (non-trivially) equivalently described via the square zero vector field

\bar{\partial}+u\partial+\{I_{BCOV},\_\}_{L=0}:\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0},

whereas the
L_{\infty}
-structure (
5.2.2
) is defined via the square zero vector field

d_{hoch}+uB+\{\rho(S^{c}_{g=0}),\_\}_{c}:\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0}.

We should be able to identify under the diagram (
5.3.20
) the shifted Poisson structures
\{\_,\_\}_{L=0}
from definition
5.3.16
respectively
\{\_,\_\}_{c}
from definition
5.2.1
, similarly for the differentials

\bar{\partial}+u\partial:\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0}

respectively

d_{hoch}+uB:\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0},

along the lines of proposition
5.3.11
.
Then the conjecture reduces to showing that under such an identification the Maurer-Cartan element

I_{BCOV}\in\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0}

is equivalent to the Maurer-Cartan element

S^{c}_{g=0}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0}

for
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}_{dg}(X)
(or again better a cyclic model of the latter via theorem 2.37 of
[
3
]
). Following theorem provides strong evidence that these two elements are indeed equivalent.

Theorem 5.3.21

(theorem 6.6 of
[
3
]
)
.

Given a smooth Frobenius associative algebra
A
, which induces a commutative Frobenius algebra structure on
HH_{*}(A)
or equivalently a closed 2d TCFT with structure maps

\omega^{A}_{g,n}:HH_{*}(A)^{\otimes n}\rightarrow k,

there is an essentially unique splitting
s^{can}
and
we have (recalling the splitting formality morphism
1.2.11
)

(\mathcal{K}_{s}\circ\rho_{A})(S^{c}_{g=0})(\lambda_{1}u^{k_{1}},\cdots,\lambda_{n}u^{k_{n}})=\omega(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\cdot\int_{\widebar{\mathcal{M}_{0,n}}}\psi^{k_{1}}\cdots\psi^{k_{n}}.

(5.3.22)

We should compare this with the formula from definition 2.10.1 of
[
31
]
for

{I_{BCOV}\in\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{E}_{BCOV}(X)\big)|_{\gamma=0}}
given by

I_{BCOV}(\mu_{1}u^{k_{1}},\cdots,\mu_{n}u^{k_{n}})=\mu_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mu_{n}\int_{\widebar{\mathcal{M}_{0,n}}}\psi^{k_{1}}\cdots\psi^{k_{n}},

(5.3.23)

furthermore in this context there is a unique splitting provided by the complex conjugate, see section 6.3 of
[
3
]
.

Remark 5.3.24
.

Let us recall sections
3.1
and
3.2
where we explain how open-closed TCFT’s induce the open and closed BD algebras and shifted Poisson algebras considered here. This makes clear that a relevant question related to conjecture
5.3.19
and more precisely related to comparing the elements (
5.3.22
) and (
5.3.23
) via above discussion is whether we can construct some (analytic version) of a (genus zero) open-closed TCFT (recalling this notion from definition
3.1.8
) such that it’s purely open sector is described by the smooth ‘cyclic’ dg-algebra of Dolbeault forms on X possibly with values in some vector bundles generating
D^{b}_{dg}(X)
and whose purely closed part is described by the ‘Frobenius’ algebra of polyvector fields with the pairing (
5.3.6
). Then we could hope to apply some version of theorem
5.3.21

47
47

47

which we cannot directly apply due to the infinite dimensional nature of these objects
(such that the algebra of Dolbeault froms would take the place of the algebra
A
from theorem
5.3.21
and such that the algebra of polyvector fields would correspond to
HH_{*}(A)
from theorem
5.3.21
), which would then imply conjecture
5.3.19
.

In fact such an analytic version of an open TCFT has been constructed in
[
37
]
in a rather general fashion; we would be interested in applying this theory to example 3.2 from
[
37
]
.

Remark 5.3.25
.

For further related questions we refer to section 8 of
[
33
]
, the paper
[
89
]
and also to proposition 8.2 of
[
62
]
.

In
[
73
,
74
]
quantum BCOV theory on the elliptic curve is constructed. Its
L\rightarrow\infty
limit defines correlation functions
48
48

48

Since an elliptic curve is compact the
L\rightarrow\infty
limit of the regularized BV Laplacian and the associated shifted Poisson bracket is zero. Thus the
{L\rightarrow\infty}
limit of the obtained solution to the QME defines mentioned correlation functions; see section 2.3 of
[
73
]
. It would be interesting to compare this phenomena with how a splitting of the non-commutative Hodge filtration is used to trivialize the BV structure in the algebraic set-up; see around equation (
1.2.11
).
, which are shown to coincide with the Gromov-Witten invariants of the mirror elliptic curve. This presents one striking application of the circle of ideas around string field theory to the topic of mirror symmetry, as laid out in the introduction in section
1.1
.

6
Large
N
Open String Field Theory

In this section we explain how to obtain shifted Poisson and BD algebra structures from cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories and their quantizations. We will see later in section
8
that we can interpret those as describing the observables of large
N
open string field theory, compare also around equation (
1.3.5
) from the introduction. In fact in order to do that we should restrict to what we call essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories, which we introduce in the last part of this section.

6.1
Shifted Poisson Structure from Cyclic
A_{\infty}
-Categories

Given a collection of (cyclic) chain complexes
V_{B}
, see definition
4.4.10
, we have that
\big(Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})[-1],d\big)
is a cochain complex; this is just definition
4.3.20
applied to the
A_{\infty}
-category with zero (higher) compositions built from
V_{B}
.
Recall that we adopted the notation

(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})\in Cyc^{*}_{+}({V_{B}})[-1],

where by definition
a_{i}\in Hom(\lambda,\mu)^{\vee}[-1]
and
a_{i+1}\in Hom(\mu,\nu)^{\vee}[-1]
for
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in B
.

Construction 6.1.1
.

We define

\{\_,\_\}:Cyc^{*}_{+}({V_{B}})[-1]\otimes Cyc^{*}_{+}({V_{B}})[-1]\rightarrow Cyc^{*}_{+}({V_{B}})[-1]

by

\{(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}),(b_{1}b_{2}\cdots b_{m}\}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle a_{j},b_{i}\rangle^{-1}(-1)^{*}(a_{i+1}\cdots a_{n}a_{1}\cdots a_{i-1}b_{j+1}\cdots b_{m}b_{1}\cdots b_{j-1})

(6.1.2)

for
n+m>2
and zero otherwise,
(-1)^{*}
being determined by the Koszul rule.
Here

\langle a_{j},b_{i}\rangle^{-1}=\begin{cases}\langle a_{j},b_{i}\rangle_{\lambda\mu}^{-1},&\text{if}\ a_{j}\in Hom(\lambda,\mu)^{\vee}[-1]\ \text{and}\ b_{i}\in Hom(\mu,\lambda)^{\vee}[-1]\ \text{for some $\mu,\lambda\in Ob(\mathcal{C})$}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Lemma 6.1.3
.

Let
V_{B}
be a collection of cyclic chain complexes of degree
d
. Then

(Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})[-1],d,\{\_,\_\})

is a
(d-2)
-shifted dg Lie algebra.

Proof.

The fact that this is a shifted Lie algebra is proven in
[
22
]
, Definition-Lemma 2.
For the algebra case see
[
58
]
, proposition 2.8 where a notation closer to here is used.
The fact that the differential is compatible with the Lie structure follows from the fact that the cyclic structure is by definition closed with respect to this differential.
∎

Recall the notion of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category from definition
4.4.4
.

Lemma 6.1.4
.

A cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category structures on
V_{B}
induces a Maurer-Cartan elements of
(Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})[-1],d,\{\_,\_\})
of degree
(3-d)
.

Proof.

This is a standard result, see eg. 4.4.3 of
[
40
]
.
Recall that we can describe a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category structure on
V_{B}
as a degree
-1

coderivation on
BarV_{B}
that squares to zero and commutes with
d
.
We get an induced differential
m_{\mathcal{C}}
on
Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})
that squares to zero and commutes with the internal differential
d
.
This fact then implies that
I_{\mathcal{C}}=\langle m_{\mathcal{C}}\_,\_\rangle
satisfies

dI_{\mathcal{C}}+\frac{1}{2}\{I_{\mathcal{C}},I_{\mathcal{C}}\}=0,

ie. it is a Maurer-Cartan element.
∎

Remark 6.1.5
.

We call such Maurer-Cartan elements also classical interaction term or
A_{\infty}
-hamiltonian.

We recall that for
V
a chain complex we use the notation
SymV:=\bigoplus_{n=0}V^{\otimes n}/S_{n}.

It is a standard fact that for
\mathfrak{g}
a dg n-shifted Lie algebra
Sym(\mathfrak{g})

becomes an n-shifted Poisson dg algebra by extending the bracket according to the Leibniz rule and the differential as a derivation.
Thus from the previous lemma it follows:

Theorem 6.1.6
.

Let
V_{B}
be a collection of cyclic chain complexes of degree
d
.
Then ‘the observables of the free
49
49

49

a name motivated by physics, where a theory coming from a quadratic action functional is referred to as free.
theory’

\mathcal{F}^{fr}(V_{B}):=Sym(Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})[-1])

are a
(d-2)
-shifted Poisson dg-algebra.

Definition 6.1.7
.

Given a cyclic
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
of degree
d
denote by

\mathcal{F}^{cl}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathcal{F}^{fr}(V_{Ob\mathcal{C}})^{tw}

the
(d-2)
-shifted Poisson dg-algebra obtained by twisting
\mathcal{F}^{fr}(V_{Ob\mathcal{C}})
by the Maurer-Cartan element
determined by cyclic
A_{\infty}
-structure through lemma
6.1.4
.

Here
cl
stands for classical.
50
50

50

a natural name from the physics perspective would also be interactive.

Lemma 6.1.8
.

We find a functor

(cyc_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}\rightarrow(Poiss_{d-2})^{op},

(F:(\mathcal{C},\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{C}})\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}))\mapsto(F^{*}:\mathcal{F}^{cl}(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{cl}(\mathcal{C}))

Proof.

Since we assume that
F
is cyclic we have commutativity of following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-28.45274ptHom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(i),F(j))^{\vee}\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(j),F(i))^{\vee}[-2d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(i),F(j))\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(j),F(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(i,j)^{\vee}\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(j,i)^{\vee}[-2d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(i,j)\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(j,i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}_{i,j}\otimes F^{*}_{j,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}^{ij}\otimes F_{*}^{ji}}

Here the horizontal two maps are defined in the standard way using the respective pairings, see section 3.1 of
[
54
]
.
Since we assume that these are non-degenerate we can invert the vertical maps and ponder commutativity of following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-28.45274ptHom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(i),F(j))^{\vee}\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(j),F(i))^{\vee}[-2d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(i),F(j))\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(j),F(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[-d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(i,j)^{\vee}\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(j,i)^{\vee}[-2d]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(i,j)\otimes Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(j,i),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}_{i,j}\otimes F^{*}_{j,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}^{ij}\otimes F_{*}^{ji}}

where now the horizontal maps are the inverses of the previous vertical maps.
Indeed, this diagram is also commutative, which follows by injectivity of the maps
F_{*}^{ji}
and commutativity of the previous diagram.
This implies that for all
F(i),F(j)\in Ob(\mathcal{D})
and
a\in Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(i),F(j))^{\vee}
and
b\in Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(F(j),F(i))^{\vee}
we have

\langle F^{*}_{ij}(a),F^{*}_{ji}(b)\rangle_{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}=\langle a,b\rangle_{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}.

As we further assume that
F
is strict it is now straightforward to verify that the induced map

F^{*}:\mathcal{F}^{cl}(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{cl}(\mathcal{C})
respects the shifted Poisson algebra structures, using definition (
6.1.2
).
∎

Lemma 6.1.9
.

Given a cyclic strict
A_{\infty}
-morphism
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}

we have that

F^{*}(I_{\mathcal{D}})=I_{\mathcal{C}}

for the associated cyclic
A_{\infty}
hamiltonians.

Proof.

This is well known, see eg. proposition 4.6 of
[
40
]
.
Concretely this can be verified using the explicit characterizations of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-functors,
equation (
4.4.9
) and equation (
4.3.9
).
∎

Remark 6.1.10
.

In the case of a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra definition 2.7 and equation 2.5 of
[
58
]

provide an alternative description of the shifted Poisson bracket. Using this alternative
description lemma 5.5 of
[
57
]
implies that the assignment of the shifted Poisson
algebra
F^{cl}(A)
to a cyclic
A_{\infty}
algebra
A
is also functorial with respect to
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-isomorphisms, not just strict ones. The authors use the so called non-commutative calculus for doing so.
See section 4.5 of
[
40
]
for a sketch of such a non-commutative calculus for the category case.
We imagine that using this one should be able to prove a similar functoriality result also for categories.
See also our remark
4.4.8
for a comparison of nomenclature used.

Remark 6.1.11
.

Similarly lemma 5.5 of
[
57
]
implies that a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism sends associated

A_{\infty}
-hamiltonian to associated
A_{\infty}
-hamiltonian. We imagine that analogous comments to
above remark apply.

Note that this discussion carries over to the full subcategory of
(cyc_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}
on
collections of cyclic chain complexes, ie. explains functoriality of theorem
6.1.6
.

6.2
Beilinson-Drinfeld Structure and Quantization of Cyclic
A_{\infty}
-Categories

Definition 6.2.1
.

Given a collection of cyclic chain complexes
V_{B}
we extend the previous dg shifted Lie
algebra structure to

Cyc^{*}(V_{B}):=Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})\Pi(\prod_{\lambda\in B}\nu_{\lambda}k)[1],

(6.2.2)

where the vector space
\prod_{\lambda\in B}\nu_{\lambda}k
spanned by the
\nu_{\lambda}
is central and we further redefine the bracket on words of length one by

\{(a),(b)\}=\begin{cases}\langle a,b\rangle^{-1}\nu_{\lambda},&\text{if}\ a\in Hom(\lambda,\lambda)^{\vee}[-1]\ \text{and}\ b_{i}\in Hom(\lambda,\lambda)^{\vee}[-1]\ \text{for some $\lambda\in Ob(\mathcal{C})$}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Remark 6.2.3
.

The image of the bracket of words of length one can contain infinite sums of
\nu_{\lambda}
over
different
\lambda
. Our definition is well defined since we are using the direct product over

B
in the central extension.

Construction 6.2.4
.

Given
V_{B}
we define

\nabla:Cyc^{*}(V_{B})[-1]\rightarrow\big(Cyc^{*}(V_{B})[-1]\otimes Cyc^{*}(V_{B})[-1]\big)_{S_{2}}

by

\nabla(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\langle a_{i},a_{j}\rangle^{-1}(-1)^{*}(a_{i+1}\cdots a_{j-1})\otimes(a_{j+1}\cdots a_{n}a_{1}\cdots a_{i-1}).

Here

\langle a_{i},a_{j}\rangle^{-1}\in k

is defined as before.
We define the value of the summand where
j=i+1
and if
a_{i}\in Hom(\beta,\lambda)^{\vee}[-1]
and

a_{j}=a_{i+1}\in Hom(\lambda,\beta)^{\vee}[-1]
to be

\langle a_{i},a_{i+1}\rangle^{-1}_{\beta\lambda}\ \nu_{\lambda}\otimes(a_{i+2}\cdots a_{n}a_{1}\cdots a_{i-1}),

otherwise zero. If additionally
n=2
we define the value by

\langle a_{i},a_{j}\rangle^{-1}_{\mu\lambda}\ \nu_{\lambda}\otimes\nu_{\mu}

if
a_{i}\in Hom(\mu,\lambda)^{\vee}[-1]
and
a_{j}=a_{i+1}\in Hom(\lambda,\mu)^{\vee}[-1]
, again
otherwise zero.
Additionally
\nabla
is zero on the vector space spanned by the
\nu
’s.

We refer to definition 2.11 of
[
58
]
for the notion of an involutive bi Lie algebra.

Lemma 6.2.5
.

Given
V_{B}
a collection of cyclic chain complexes of degree
d
we have that

(Cyc^{*}(V_{B}),d,\{\_,\_\},\nabla)
is an involutive bi Lie dg-algebra of degree
(d-2)
.

Proof.

This is proved in theorem 7 of
[
22
]
. Note however that the author does not consider
the central extension. One can check directly that the result remains true. For the one object
case this is also explained in section 3.2 of
[
54
]
, where this central extension is included.
∎

From now on let us
restrict to odd
d
:
Introducing
\gamma
, a formal variable of cohomological degree
6-2d
, we denote

\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B}):=Sym\big(Cyc^{*}(V_{B})[d-4]\big)\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket[3-d]

where
V_{B}
is a collection of cyclic chain complexes of degree
d
.
Denote by
\delta
the Chevalley-Eilenberg differential extended to
\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B})
from the Lie algebra chain
complex of the odd Lie algebra
Cyc^{*}(V_{B})
.
Denote by
d
and
\nabla
the differentials extended
from
Cyc^{*}(V_{B})
as derivations to
\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B})
and by
\{\_,\_\}

the shifted Poisson bracket extended from the odd Lie bracket on
Cyc^{*}(V_{B})
.

Theorem 6.2.6
.

Let
V_{B}
be a collection of cyclic chain complexes of degree
d
. Then

\Big(\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B}),d+\nabla+\gamma\delta,\{\_,\_\}\Big)

is a
(d-2)
twisted Beilinson-Drinfeld algebra over
k\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket
.
\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B})
has multiplication of even degree
(d-3)
.

The superscript
pq
stands for prequantum.
51
51

51

A better name should have been derived from
‘quantization of free theory’, which is what
\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V)
is.

Proof.

The result follows as in lemma 3.4 of
[
58
]
from our lemma
6.2.5
and accounting for
the shifts made.
Note that these shifts and the degree of
\gamma
precisely give the correct
degrees required in our definition of an
r
-twisted BD algebra, here for
s=(d-2)
.
∎

Given a strict cyclic
A_{\infty}
-morphism
F:V_{B}\rightarrow V_{C}
we define a map

F^{*}:Cyc^{*}(V_{C})\rightarrow Cyc^{*}(V_{B})

by setting

F^{*}(\nu_{\lambda})=\sum_{\mu\in F^{-1}(\lambda)}\nu_{\mu}.

One can check directly that this is compatible with the extension of the odd Lie bracket.
Furthermore, by arguments analogous to the discussion of lemma
6.1.8
, it follows that
F^{*}
intertwines the co Lie brackets.
We denote by the same letter the extension of this map to

F^{*}:\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B}),

which is a map of BD algebras over
k\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket
. As an upshot we find

Lemma 6.2.7
.

There is a functor from the full subcategory of collections of cyclic chain complexes within the category

(cyc_{d}A_{\infty}Cat)_{st}
to the category of
(d-2)
twisted BD algebras by the assignment

\mathcal{F}^{pq}:\ (F:V_{B}\rightarrow V_{C})\mapsto\big(F^{*}:\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B})\big).

Definition 6.2.8
.

Define

p:\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B})\rightarrow Sym^{1}(Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})[d-4])[3-d]\cong Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})[-1]\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{fr}(V_{B}),

where the first map is the projection on the ‘
Sym^{1}
,
\gamma=0
and
\nu=0
part’.
The last map is just the inclusion.

It is straightforward to check that
p
is a dequantization map in the sense of definition
4.1.17
.
For example we have
p(\gamma\delta)=0=p(\nabla).

Definition 6.2.9
.

Given a collection of cyclic chain complexes
V_{B}
and
I\in MCE(\mathcal{F}^{fr}(V_{B}))
, we say that

I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B}))

of degree
(3-d)
is a
quantization of
I
if
p(I^{q})=I.

Remark 6.2.10
.

Note that more generally one may ask a quantization to be a differential
d^{q}
on the underlying
shifted Poisson algebra of
\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V)
satisfying the BD relation and such
p\circ d^{q}=d^{cl}\circ p.

Thus we are more restrictive, imposing
d^{q}=d+\nabla+\gamma\delta+\{I^{q},\_\}
.
We ask that a quantization
I^{q}
of the interacting theory
\mathcal{F}^{cl}
is compatible with the standard quantization of the free theory, i.e. with
\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V)
.

Remark 6.2.11
.

Quantizations of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories are sometimes called quantum
A_{\infty}
-categories.
Equivalently a Maurer-Cartan element
I^{q}\in\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{\Lambda})
may be described as a possibly curved (involutive bi-Lie
)_{\infty}
-algebra structure on a central extension of cyclic cochains of this category. Compare work by Campos-Merkulov-Willwacher
[
20
]
, section 5 and also work by Cieliebak-Fukaya-Latschev
[
25
]
, section 12 and work by Naef-Willwacher
[
82
]
, section 7. In
[
87
]
Slawinski has explicitly analyzed the lift of the
A_{\infty}
-structure on the Fukaya category of an elliptic curve to a quantum
A_{\infty}
-structure by using a certain combinatorial model.
However, these authors don’t consider the central extension, which is crucial for us.

Remark 6.2.12
.

Further we restrict to the case where
I^{q}
has zero constant terms, that is its components in the ground field and the vector space spanned by the
\nu
’s are zero.

Given
F:V_{B}\rightarrow V_{C}
a cyclic strict
A_{\infty}
-morphism between collections of cyclic chain complexes and an
I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{C}))
then

F^{*}I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B})).

Indeed this follows as we saw that
F^{*}
is compatible with the odd Lie bracket,
thus also with the induced Chevalley Eilenberg differential, as well as the odd co Lie bracket.

Thus we can define a category which we denote

(\text{q-cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st},

whose objects are pairs of a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
together with an

I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{Ob\mathcal{C}}))
such that
p(I^{q})=I_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Its morphisms are cyclic strict
A_{\infty}
-morphisms
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
such that
F^{*}I^{q}_{\mathcal{D}}=I^{q}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
Note that there is a forgetful functor to
(cyc_{d}A_{\infty}Cat)_{st}
induced by the dequantization map
p
.

Remark 6.2.13
.

In the literature such elements
I^{q}
have also been referred to as quantum
A_{\infty}
-categories.

Definition 6.2.14
.

Given an object of
(\text{q-cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}
define

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{q}(\mathcal{C}):=\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B})^{tw}}

as the
(d-2)
twisted BD algebra obtained by twisting
\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{B})
by the MC element
I^{q}
.
The assignment
6.2.14
defines a functor

\mathcal{F}^{q}:\ (\text{q-cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}\rightarrow(\text{BD}^{(d-2)tw})^{op},

where on morphisms we use
F^{*}
.

Remark 6.2.15
.

Note that this notation for
\mathcal{F}^{q}(\mathcal{C})
is ambiguous and we need to have the choice of an
I^{q}
in mind.

In this work we did not examine how the discussion so far can be refined when considering homotopy equivalent solutions to the quantum master equation.
Compare section 6 of
[
54
]
.

6.3
Essentially Finite Cyclic
A_{\infty}
-Categories

Recall that given a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
we defined the central extension of our previous shifted Lie algebra by

Cyc^{*}(V_{B}):=Cyc^{*}_{+}(V_{B})\times(\prod_{\lambda\in B}\nu_{\lambda}k[1]).

As we will see in section
8.2.4
, under the ‘quantized’ LQT map at level
N
, each
\nu_{\lambda}
will be send to the actual number
N
.
However, if we allow infinite sums of
\nu_{\lambda}
’s this would be ill-defined.
Thus we restrict ourselves as follows:

Definition 6.3.1
.

We call a cyclic unital
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
that admits a finite skeleton
Sk\mathcal{C}
, that is a subcategory with finitely many objects whose inclusion defines an equivalence,
an
essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
.

Remark 6.3.2
.

Note that for an essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category the inclusion

Sk\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

is strict and cyclic.

We define a category, denoted

(\text{e.f cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st},

as the full subcategory of
(cyc_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}
given by essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories.

In practise we will work with a slightly more complicated, though equivalent category:

Definition 6.3.3
.

We denote by

(\text{e.f}^{+}\text{cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st},

the category whose objects are essentially finite cyclic unital
A_{\infty}
-categories together with the choice of a finite skeleton and together with the inclusion

Sk\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C},

and whose morphisms are diagrams as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{D},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(6.3.4)

where
F
is a strict cyclic
A_{\infty}
-functor.

Note that the obvious functor

(\text{e.f}^{+}\text{cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}\rightarrow(\text{e.f cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}

(6.3.5)

is an equivalence, which is why we will treat the two appearing categories as interchangeable. We will later see (
6.3.9
) that different choices of an inverse of (
6.3.5
), that is different choices of a finite skeleta for each essentially finite category, are related by natural quasi-isomorphism, once we localized appropriately.

Let us denote by
\mathcal{D}Poiss_{(d-2)}
the localization of the category of

(d-2)
shifted Poisson algebras with respect to quasi-isomorphisms.
We (re)define previous functors:

Lemma 6.3.6
.

Sending
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C})
an essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
category of degree
d
to
\mathcal{F}^{cl}(Sk\mathcal{C})
defines a functor

\mathcal{F}^{cl}_{fin}:\ (\text{e.f cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}\rightarrow(\mathcal{D}Poiss_{(d-2)})^{op}.

This follows directly from definition
6.3.4
and (
6.3.8
) below.
Further by (
6.3.9
) we will see that this functor is independent of the choice of skeleton, up to isomorphism.
To see these facts we first remark the standard result

Lemma 6.3.7
.

Cyclic (co)homology is invariant under
A_{\infty}
-equivalence.

Proof.

As a sketch, by Proposition 7.3.6 and Lemma 7.4.1 of
[
36
]
it follows that Hochschild (co)homology is invariant under
A_{\infty}
-equivalence.
By arguments as in Loday, corollary 2.2.4, it follows that also cyclic (co)homology is invariant.
∎

Thus given
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C})
an essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category we get a map of shifted Poisson algebras

\mathcal{F}^{cl}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{cl}(Sk\mathcal{C}),

(6.3.8)

whose underlying map of chain complexes is a quasi-isomorphism.
This follows from the lemma before, as the inclusion
Sk\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is in particular an
A_{\infty}
-equivalence.

Furthermore (
6.3.8
) defines a natural quasi-isomorphism as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{e.f cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}}
(\mathcal{D}Poiss_{(d-2)})^{op}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(cyc_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{cl}_{fin}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{cl}}

which we remark to justify our redefinition.
Regarding the choice of finite skeleton of a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category we remark that
given a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
with two choices of finite skeleta
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C}_{1})
,
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C}_{2})
we get a zig-zag of shifted Poisson algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{cl}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{cl}(Sk\mathcal{C}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{cl}(Sk\mathcal{C}_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(6.3.9)

where the underlying maps of chain complexes are quasi-isomorphisms.

We make similar (re)definitions for the quantized context:

Definition 6.3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category.
Denote by
I
the
A_{\infty}
-hamiltonian associated to
\mathcal{C}
.
We say that

I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{Ob{\mathcal{C}}}))

is
a quantization of
\mathcal{C}

if

p(I^{q})=I,

recalling the dequantization maps p from definition
6.2.8
.

Definition 6.3.11
.

We define a category, denoted

(\text{q.e.f. cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}.

Its objects are quantizations of essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories.
The morphisms are given by morphisms of the underlying cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories

F:\ \mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}

(6.3.12)

such that additionally
F^{*}I^{q}=J^{q}
for the respective quantizations.

In practice we will work with a slightly more complicated, though equivalent category:

Definition 6.3.13
.

We define a category, denoted

(\text{q.e.f}^{+}\text{cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}.

An objects of this category is given by

•

an essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category

•

together with the choice of a finite skeleton and with the data of inclusion

\Phi:Sk\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C},

•

denoting by
(I_{f},I)
the pair of
A_{\infty}
-hamiltonian associated to
Sk\mathcal{C}
and the one associated to
\mathcal{C}
, we further ask for a pair of

I^{q}_{f}\in MCE(\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{Ob{Sk\mathcal{C}}}))\ \text{and}\ I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{F}^{pq}(V_{Ob{\mathcal{C}}}))

such that

p(I^{q}_{f})=I_{f}\ \text{and}\ \ p(I^{q})=I,

(recalling the dequantization maps p from definition
6.2.8
) and we have that

\Phi^{*}I^{q}=I^{q}_{f}.

The morphisms are given by morphisms in
(\text{e.f}^{+}\text{cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}
, that is diagrams like

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{D},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(6.3.14)

such that additionally
F^{*}I^{q}=J^{q}
for the respective quantizations.

Note that the obvious functor

(\text{q.e.f}^{+}\text{cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}\rightarrow(\text{q.e.f cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}

(6.3.15)

is an equivalence, which is why we will treat the two appearing categories as interchangeable.

Note that given an element of
(\text{q.e.f}^{+}\text{cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}
the induced map

\Phi^{*}:\mathcal{F}^{q}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{q}(Sk\mathcal{C})

(6.3.16)

of BD algebras is a quasi-isomorphism.
Indeed, consider the complete and exhaustive filtration of
\mathcal{F}^{q}(\mathcal{C})
described in example 6.3 of
[
54
]
.
The map
\Phi^{*}
is by definition a filtered map.
Then the claim follows from the from the Eilenberg-Moore comparison theorem (theorem 5.5.11 of
[
94
]
) as on the first page of the spectral sequence associated to the filtrations the map
\Phi^{*}
induces a quasi-isomorphism, as remarked in (
6.3.8
).

We denote by
\mathcal{D}\text{BD}^{(d-2)tw}
the category obtained by localizing
\text{BD}^{(d-2)tw}
with respect to quasi-isomorphisms.
Since (
6.3.16
) is an quasi-isomorphism and by definition (
6.3.14
) we observe that
sending a quantization of an essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category to
\mathcal{F}^{q}(Sk\mathcal{C})
defines a functor

\mathcal{F}^{q}_{fin}:\ (\text{q.e.f. cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}\rightarrow(\mathcal{D}\text{BD}^{(d-2)tw})^{op}.

(6.3.17)

This redefinition makes sense as follows: Given a quantization of an essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C})
we have by
6.3.16
a map of BD algebras

\mathcal{F}^{q}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{q}(Sk\mathcal{C}),

whose underlying map of chain complexes is a quasi-isomorphism. This defines a natural quasi-isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{q.e.f.cyc}_{d}A_{\infty})_{st}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{D}\text{BD}^{(d-2)tw})^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{q.cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{q}_{fin}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{q}}

Regarding the dependence on how a quantum
A_{\infty}
-category is essentially finitely presented we note:
Given a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
which is finitely presented in two ways
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C}_{1})
,
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C}_{2})
and with respective quantizations that coincide on
\mathcal{C}
we get a zig-zag of BD algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{q}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{q}(Sk\mathcal{C}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{q}(Sk\mathcal{C}_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(6.3.18)

where the underlying maps of chain complexes are quasi-isomorphisms.
Thus the functor (
6.3.17
) does not depend on the choice of skeleton and compatible quantization, up to isomorphism.

7
From (Cyclic)
A_{\infty}
-Categories to
(Cyclic)
L_{\infty}
-Algebras

In this section we recall the standard construction of shifted Poisson and BD algebras given a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra or a quantization of such as input. Next we study a functor from
A_{\infty}
-categories to
A_{\infty}
-algebras, which lands in
L_{\infty}
-algebras when composed with the standard commutator functor. We examine how this functor behaves when additionally considering cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories, for which we restrict ourselves to essentially finite categories.

7.1
Shifted Poisson Structure

A cyclic chain complex
V
is by definition a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra for which
l=0
; see definition
4.2.4
.
Given a cyclic chain complex
V
we denote

\mathcal{O}^{fr}(V):=C^{*}_{+}(V),

recalling definition
4.2.9
of the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex.
By extending the inverse of the pairing of
V

\langle\_,\_\rangle^{-1}:V^{\vee}[-1]\otimes V^{\vee}[-1]\rightarrow k[d-2],

according to the Leibniz rule to the symmetric algebra underlying the Lie algebra cochains we can define a
(d-2)
-shifted Poisson structure.
The differential is compatible with the shifted Poisson bracket because
\langle\_,\_\rangle
is closed with respect to the differential.
Summarizing (for details see section 3.1 of
[
54
]
) we have

Theorem 7.1.1
.

Let
(V,d,\langle\_,\_\rangle)
be a cyclic chain complex of degree
d
. Then

\big(\mathcal{O}^{fr}(V),d,\{\_,\_\}\big)

is a
(d-2)
-shifted Poisson dg-algebra.

Lemma 7.1.2
.

A cyclic
L_{\infty}
-structures on a cyclic chain complex
V
induces a Maurer-Cartan elements of
\mathcal{O}^{fr}(V)
of degree
(3-d)
.

Proof.

This is a standard result and follows as in the
A_{\infty}
-case.
The Maurer-Cartan element is given by
I_{L}:=\langle l\_,\_\rangle.

∎

Definition 7.1.3
.

Given
L
a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra with underlying cyclic chain complex of degree d denote by
\mathcal{O}^{cl}(L)
the
(d-2)
-shifted Poisson algebra obtained by twisting
\mathcal{O}^{fr}(V)
by the MCE determined by lemma
7.1.2
.

Denote by
(cyc_{d}L_{\infty}alg)_{st}
the category whose objects are cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebras of degree
d
and whose morphisms are cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-morphisms.

Lemma 7.1.4
.

We find a functor

(cyc_{d}L_{\infty}Alg)_{st}\rightarrow(Poiss_{(d-2)})^{op}

\big(\phi:(L_{1}\rightarrow L_{2})\big)\mapsto\big(\phi^{*}:\mathcal{O}^{cl}(L_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{cl}(L_{1})\big).

Proof.

Indeed, this can be verified as in the
A_{\infty}
-case. See lemma
6.1.8
.
∎

7.2
Beilinson-Drinfeld Structure

From now on let us restrict again to
d
odd.
We introduce
\hbar
, a formal variable of cohomological degree
3-d
.
For
(V,d,\langle\_,\_\rangle)
a cyclic chain complex denote

\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V):=C^{*}(V)\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket,

the non-reduced Chevalley-Eilenberg complex adjoined
\hbar
.
Eg. following
[
54
]
, we can define a BD differential
d^{q}
on
\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V)
as

d^{q}=d+\hbar\Delta.

Indeed, by imposing the BD-relation (
4.1.3
) and
\hbar
-linearity we only need to specify the values of
d^{q}
on
C^{2}(V)
, which leaves us to specify the value of
\Delta
on
C^{2}(V).

It is given by
\Delta(a\cdot b)=\langle a,b\rangle^{-1}
for
a,b\in L^{\vee}[-1]
.
This is compatible with defining
\{\_,\_\}
by
\hbar
-linearly extending the bracket on
\mathcal{O}^{fr}(V)
.
Summarizing we have

Theorem 7.2.1
.

For
V
a cyclic chain complex

\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V):=\big(C^{*}(V)\llbracket\hbar\rrbracket,d^{q},\{\_,\_\}\big)

is a
(d-2)
twisted BD algebra over
k
.

Again, analogously to the
A_{\infty}
-case it follows:

Lemma 7.2.2
.

The assignement

(\phi:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2})\mapsto(\phi^{*}:\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V_{1}))

defines a functor from the full subcategory of cyclic chain complexes of degree
d
of

(cyc_{d}L_{\infty}alg)_{st}
to the category of (d-2) twisted BD algebras.

Definition 7.2.3
.

Define

p:\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{fr}(V)

by setting
\hbar
and the
n=0
factor of the Chevalley-Eilenberg cochains to zero.
It is easy to verify that this defines a dequantization map in the sense of definition
4.1.17
.

Definition 7.2.4
.

We say that an

I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V))
of degree
(3-d)
is a
quantization
of
I\in MCE(\mathcal{O}^{fr}(V))
if
p(I^{q})=I
.

If
\phi:V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}
is a strict cyclic
L_{\infty}
-morphism of cyclic chain complexes and if
I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V_{2}))
then
\phi^{*}I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V_{1}))
.
This allows us to define a category, which we denote by

(\text{q-cyc}_{d}L_{\infty}alg)_{st}

whose objects are pairs of a cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L
with underlying chain complex V and
I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V))
such that
p(I^{q})=I
.
Its morphisms are cyclic strict
L_{\infty}
-morphisms

\phi:\ L_{1}\rightarrow L_{2}

such that
\phi^{*}I^{q}_{2}=I^{q}_{1}.

Definition 7.2.5
.

Given a cyclic
L_{\infty}
algebra
L
denote by
V
its underlying chain complex and by
I\in MCE(\mathcal{O}^{fr}(V))
its associated
L_{\infty}
-hamiltonian.
Assume we are given a quantization
I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V)),
then we define

\mathcal{O}^{q}(L):=\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V)^{tw}

as the twist of
\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V)
by the MCE
I^{q}.

This assignment defines a functor

\mathcal{O}^{q}:(\text{q-cyc}_{d}L_{\infty}Alg)_{st}\rightarrow(\text{BD}^{(d-2)tw})^{op}.

Remark 7.2.6
.

Note that the notation
\mathcal{O}^{q}(L)
is ambiguous and we implicitly have to keep in mind an
I^{q}\in MCE(\mathcal{O}^{pq}(V))
.

7.3
Commutator
L_{\infty}
-algebras of
A_{\infty}
-categories

We recall the standard definition (eg definition 2.8 of
[
54
]
)
of the functor

[\_,\_]:A_{\infty}Alg\rightarrow L_{\infty}Alg,

(7.3.1)

called the commutator functor, which generalizes the construction of the commutator Lie algebra out of an associative algebra.

In this section we wish to generalize the domain of this functor to
A_{\infty}
-categories.
We do so by defining a functor

\ A_{\infty}cat\rightarrow A_{\infty}Alg.

(F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D})\mapsto(\tilde{F}:A_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow A_{\mathcal{D}}).

On objects the functor is defined as follows:

Construction 7.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category.
Recall that this defines the datum of a degree
-1
coderivation
m_{\mathcal{C}}
on its bar complex
Bar\mathcal{C}
that squares to zero.

•

We set

A_{\mathcal{C}}:=\bigoplus_{(\lambda,\mu)\in Ob(\mathcal{C})^{\times 2}}Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda,\mu).

(7.3.3)

•

We define an
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
A_{\mathcal{C}}
as follows.
Define maps

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{86.30304pt}{3.87164pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here
\iota_{n}
denotes the projection onto length
n
tensor factors of the form in the bar complex - that is those with ‘matching boundary conditions’, except for the first and last one.
52
52

52

See equation (
4.3.1
).

Further

\pi:Bar\mathcal{C}\rightarrow A_{\mathcal{C}}[1]

denotes projection to tensors of
Bar\mathcal{C}
of length one.
Its image is by definition
A_{\mathcal{C}}[1]
.

•

We extend the maps
m^{n}
as a coderivation to
Bar(A_{\mathcal{C}})
, which we denote by
m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}
and which has degree
-1
.
Thus it remains to show that
m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}^{2}=0
to prove that this defines an
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
{A_{\mathcal{C}}}
.

•

We observe that the
\iota_{n}
maps induce a chain map

\iota:Bar(A_{\mathcal{C}})\rightarrow Bar\mathcal{C}.

Note that this map has a ‘canonical’ complement
W
to its kernel, given by tensors with‘matching boundary conditions, except the first and last one’.
By definition
\iota|_{W}
is injective.

•

It follows by the definition of
m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}
that
\iota\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}=m_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\iota
.
Thus we have that

\iota\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}^{2}=m_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}\circ\iota=0.

It holds that
m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}(W)\subseteq W.
Thus since
\iota
is injective on
W
, it follows that
{m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}^{2}|_{W}=0}
.
However, by definition also
m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}|_{ker(\iota)}=0
, thus also
m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}^{2}|_{ker(\iota)}=0.

Since
W
is complementary to
{ker(\iota)}
this together implies

m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}^{2}=0

in general.

Definition 7.3.4
.

Given
\mathcal{C}
an
A_{\infty}
-category denote by

\big(A_{\mathcal{C}},d+m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}\big)
the thus constructed
A_{\infty}
-algebra, where
d
denotes the internal differential.

Next we define the functor on morphisms:

Construction 7.3.5
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a functor of
A_{\infty}
-categories which by definition gives a map of coalgebras

Bar\mathcal{C}\rightarrow Bar\mathcal{D}.

•

We define

\tilde{F}_{*}:Bar(A_{\mathcal{C}})\rightarrow Bar(A_{\mathcal{D}})

by extending

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{F}_{*}:Bar(A_{\mathcal{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bar\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bar\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\mathcal{D}}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{D}}}

as a map of coalgebras.
It has degree zero.
We check that
\tilde{F}_{*}
intertwines the coderivation
m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}
with
m_{A_{\mathcal{D}}}
:

•

We have a commutative diagram of chain complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bar\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bar\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bar(A_{\mathcal{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bar(A_{\mathcal{D}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{F}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{D}}}

(7.3.6)

which follows by construction.

•

Thus we have that

\iota_{\mathcal{D}}\circ\tilde{F}_{*}\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}={F}_{*}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{C}}\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}={F}_{*}\circ m_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{C}}=m_{\mathcal{D}}\circ{F}_{*}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{C}}=m_{\mathcal{D}}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{D}}\circ\tilde{F}_{*}=\iota_{\mathcal{D}}\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{D}}}\circ\tilde{F}_{*},

where we first used commutativity of above diagram, then that
\iota_{\mathcal{C}}
intertwines the respective coderivations.
Then we used that
F_{*}
intertwines the coderivations, next again commutatvity of above diagram and lastly that
\iota_{\mathcal{D}}
intertwines the respective coderivations.
Thus

\iota_{\mathcal{D}}\circ\tilde{F}_{*}\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}=\iota_{\mathcal{D}}\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{D}}}\circ\tilde{F}_{*}.

•

We have

\tilde{F}_{*}\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}|_{ker(\iota_{\mathcal{C}})}=0=m_{A_{\mathcal{D}}}\circ\tilde{F}_{*}|_{ker(\iota_{\mathcal{C}})},

which follows by definition.
On the other hand we have that

\tilde{F}_{*}\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}(W_{\mathcal{C}})\subseteq W_{\mathcal{D}}\ \text{and}\ \ m_{A_{\mathcal{D}}}\circ\tilde{F}_{*}(W_{\mathcal{C}})\subseteq W_{\mathcal{D}}.

Since
\iota_{\mathcal{D}}|_{W_{\mathcal{D}}}
is injective we conclude from the previous line and the previous bullet point that

m_{A_{\mathcal{D}}}\circ\tilde{F}_{*}=\tilde{F}_{*}\circ m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}}.

•

Unwinding the definitions one can also show that
\widetilde{G\circ F}=\tilde{G}\circ\tilde{F}.

Lemma 7.3.7
.

Constructions
7.3.2
and
7.3.5
allow us to define

(\tilde{\_}):A_{\infty}cat\rightarrow A_{\infty}Alg.

(F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D})\mapsto(\tilde{F}:A_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow A_{\mathcal{D}}).

Remark 7.3.8
.

Note that the chain map
\iota
is not a map of coalgebras.
However, one may ponder whether it is induced from a morphism in a certain bi-module category, see section 2.2.11 of
[
76
]
.

Let us make observations that we will need in the next section.
By restricting
\iota_{\mathcal{C}}
to cyclic words we get an induced map:

\iota_{\mathcal{C}}:(Cyc_{*}^{+}(A_{\mathcal{C}})[1],d+m_{A_{\mathcal{C}}})\rightarrow(Cyc_{*}^{+}(\mathcal{C})[1],d+m_{\mathcal{C}}).

(7.3.9)

Additionally to the functor (
7.3.1
) we also get a map

(C_{*}^{+}(L),d+l)\rightarrow(Cyc_{*}^{+}(A)[1],d+m_{A}),

(7.3.10)

where
L
is the commutator
L_{\infty}
-algebra of
A
,
see equation 2.6 of
[
54
]
.
Both these maps are functorial with respect to
A_{\infty}
-functors.

7.4
Cyclic Commutator
L_{\infty}
-Algebras

Given a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category we would like to associate to it a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra, generalizing the previous section.
The relatively strict notions we use mean that we restrict ourselves to essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories to do so.

Construction 7.4.1
.

Let
\big((\mathcal{C},\langle\_,\_\rangle_{\mathcal{C}}),(Sk\mathcal{C},\langle\_,\_\rangle_{Sk\mathcal{C}})\big)
be an essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category.
Then by the previous section
{A_{Sk\mathcal{C}}}
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra.
Furthermore we define a symmetric non-degenerate chain map

\langle\_,\_\rangle_{A_{Sk\mathcal{C}}}:\ A_{Sk\mathcal{C}}\otimes A_{Sk\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow k[-d]

by
recalling that

A_{Sk\mathcal{C}}=\bigoplus_{(\lambda,\mu)\in Ob(Sk\mathcal{C})^{\times 2}}Hom_{Sk\mathcal{C}}(\lambda,\mu)
and by linearly extending

\langle x,y\rangle_{A_{Sk\mathcal{C}}}=\begin{cases}\langle x,y\rangle_{Sk\mathcal{C}},&\text{if}\ x\in Hom_{Sk\mathcal{C}}(\lambda,\mu)\text{and}\ y\in Hom_{Sk\mathcal{C}}(\mu,\lambda)\ \text{for some}\ \mu,\lambda\in Sk\mathcal{C}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(7.4.2)

Non-degeneracy follows by non-degeneracy of the original pairing and since the cardinality of
Ob(Sk\mathcal{C})
is finite.
By noting that

\langle m_{n}(\_,\cdots,\_),\_\rangle_{A_{Sk\mathcal{C}}}=\iota_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(\langle m_{n}(\_,\cdots,\_),\_\rangle_{Sk\mathcal{C}})\in Cyc^{*}_{+}(A_{Sk\mathcal{C}})

we conclude that we indeed defined a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra.

We explain how to associate a morphism of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras to a strict cyclic functor of essentially finite
A_{\infty}
-categories:

Construction 7.4.3
.

Given
F
a strict cyclic
A_{\infty}
-functor of essentially finite
A_{\infty}
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{D},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(7.4.4)

we note that by defining
\widebar{\mathcal{D}}
to be the full subcategory of the union of
Sk\mathcal{D}
and the image of
Sk\mathcal{C}
we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widebar{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{D},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(7.4.5)

where all the arrows are strict cyclic
A_{\infty}
-functors.
Further
\widebar{\mathcal{D}}
also has only finitely many objects and the functor

Sk\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\widebar{\mathcal{D}}

is an equivalence.
This is because the induced homology functor is full and faithful by definition.
Further it is essentially surjective since every object of
\mathcal{D}
is isomorphic to an object in
Sk\mathcal{D}
, thus in particular every object of
\widebar{\mathcal{D}}
as a full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
.
Summarizing, given a strict cyclic functor of essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories as (
7.4.5
), we obtain a roof

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widebar{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{D},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(7.4.6)

where all functors are strict and cyclic and injective on objects.

Let us denote by
W
the class of morphisms of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras inducing isomorphisms
on cyclic cohomology.
53
53

53

Since we are working over a characteristic zero field we could equivalently consider cyclic homology or Hochschild homology.

We find

Lemma 7.4.7
.

The assignment
7.4.1
extends to a functor

(\tilde{\_})_{fin}:\ (\text{e.f. cyc}_{d}A_{\infty}cat)_{st}\rightarrow(cyc_{d}A_{\infty}Alg)_{st}[W^{-1}]

(7.4.8)

sending equivalences to isomorphisms.

In particular, see (
7.4.10
), this functor is up to isomorphism independent from the choice of a skeleton.

Proof.

Given a strict cyclic functor of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories with finitely many objects which is additionally injective on objects we obtain
a strict cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra morphism of the induced cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras (from construction
7.4.1
), as can be deduced easily.
Furthermore given a functor which is an equivalence, we already saw that it induces an isomorphism on cyclic cohomology.
We will see later in the proof of theorem
8.1.3
that the cyclic cohomology of an
A_{\infty}
-category and its associated cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra are isomorphic.
Thus from diagram (
7.4.6
) it follows that the assignment on morphisms underlying (
7.4.8
) is well defined.
It remains to verify functoriality.
Given

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

strict cyclic functors of essentially finite cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories. Then we need to compare the images of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{E},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\circ H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widebar{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widebar{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{E},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

under the assignment
7.4.8
.
Let us denote by
\mathcal{E}^{*}
the full subcategory of
\mathcal{E}
on the union of
Sk\mathcal{E}
and the images of
Sk\mathcal{D}
and
Sk\mathcal{C}
.
Then the images of both diagrams before are, in the localized category, equal to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Sk\mathcal{E},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\circ H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

which proves that they are equal, which proves functoriality
∎

Remark 7.4.9
.

Further, diagram (
7.4.5
) induces a natural quasi-isomorphism between the functor
7.4.8
, composed with forgetting the cyclic structure,
and the functor induced by sending an essentially finite
A_{\infty}
-category
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C})
to
\mathcal{C}
and then applying the functor
7.3.7
.

Lastly, given an cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
which is finitely presented in two ways

(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C}_{1})
,
(\mathcal{C},Sk\mathcal{C}_{2})
we get a roof of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\widebar{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Sk\mathcal{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{Sk\mathcal{C}_{2}},}

(7.4.10)

where the arrows are in
W
, which tells us that the choice of finite presentation in lemma
