12.3.1
An Interlude on Graphs

In this subsection we prove theorem
12.3.14
, which is essential in the proof of the key lemma
12.3.8
.

Construction 12.3.9
.

Given a connected marked graph
G
with
m-1
vertices
and a partition
I\sqcup J=h_{1}
of the half-edges
h_{1}
belonging to the (for example) first vertex of the graph we can construct a new marked graph with
m
vertices
where now only the half-edges
I
belong to the first vertex and the half-edges
J
are declared to belong to a new vertex, marked
m
.
We denote the new graph by
G^{I,J}
.
However, we need to be careful as in this process we may create two disconnected marked graph, denoted
G^{I}
respectively
G^{J}
,
which for instance always happens when our starting graph is a star. See the proof of theorem
12.3.14
below for how we mark these new graphs.

We will see that we are also able to reverse this procedure. To make this more precise we introduce the following notations.

Definition 12.3.10
.

For a given marked graphs
(G,f)
and two partitions of their half-edges belonging to the first vertex
I_{1}\sqcup J_{1}=h_{1}
respectively
I_{2}\sqcup J_{2}=h_{1}
we say

(I_{1},J_{1})\sim(I_{2},J_{2})\ \ \text{if}\ \begin{cases}G^{I_{1},J_{1}}\cong G^{I_{2},J_{2}},&\text{if the resulting graphs are connected}\\
G^{I_{1}}\cong G^{I_{2}}\ \text{and}\ G^{J_{1}}\cong G^{J_{2}},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

To be precise the isomorphism have to be from the category of marked graphs.
Further given a graph
(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m-1}
and
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
denote by

P_{(G,f)}^{k_{1},k_{2}}:=\{I\sqcup J=h_{1}\ |\ |I|=k_{1},|J|=k_{2}\}/\sim.

Definition 12.3.11
.

Fix
0\leq g
,
n<0
and
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. We call
A_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}

the set of isomorphism classes of marked graphs with a partition of the first vertex
,
B_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}

the set of isomorphism classes of marked graphs with fixed valencies at two vertices
and
C_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}

the set of 2-disconnected isomorphism classes of marked graphs with a partition
. More precisely:

\displaystyle A_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}:=

\displaystyle\{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m-1},I,J\in P_{(G,f)}^{k_{1},k_{2}}\},

\displaystyle B_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}:=

\displaystyle\{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m}\ |\ |h_{1}|=k_{1},|h_{m}|=k_{2}\}

\displaystyle C_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}:=

\displaystyle\{(G_{1},f_{1})\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g_{1},n_{1})}_{m_{1}},(G_{2},f_{2})\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g_{2},n_{2})}_{m_{2}},\ L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=\{1,\cdots,m\}|1\in L_{1},m\in L_{2}

\displaystyle\ g_{1}+g_{2}=g,m_{1}+m_{2}=m,n_{1}+n_{2}=n,|h_{1}(G_{1})|=k_{1},|h_{m}(G_{2})|=k_{2}\}.

Further given
1\leq i<j\leq m
we denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}:=

\displaystyle\{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m}\ |\ |h_{i}|=k_{1},|h_{j}|=k_{2}\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}:=

\displaystyle\{(G_{1},f_{1})\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g_{1},n_{1})}_{m_{1}},(G_{2},f_{2})\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g_{2},n_{2})}_{m_{2}},\ L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=\{1,\cdots,m\}|i\in L_{1},j\in L_{2}

\displaystyle\ g_{1}+g_{2}=g,m_{1}+m_{2}=m,n_{1}+n_{2}=n,|h_{i}(G_{1})|=k_{1},|h_{j}(G_{2})|=k_{2}\}

Obviously we have that

{B}_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}\cong\widetilde{B}_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}

(12.3.12)

{C}_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}\cong\widetilde{C}_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}

(12.3.13)

by swapping
1
with
i
and
m
with
j
.

Theorem 12.3.14
.

Fix
0\leq g
,
0<n
and
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there is a bijection

\psi:A_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}\cong B_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}\cup C_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}.

(12.3.15)

Proof.

We have already described in construction
12.3.9
how given an element of
A_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}
we produce (a) graph(s) on the right hand side. In the connected case we also described the marking. In the disconnected case we simply use the old marking, but reorder according to size. Further in the disconnected case the partition
L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=\{1,\cdots,m\}
is obtained by remembering which of the previous marked vertices are now part of
G_{1}
(including the old first one) and which of the previous marked vertices are now part of
G_{2}
(including the new last one, which was obtained by splitting the old first one into two).

Given an element in the right hand side (be it connected or not), whose total number of vertices is
m
, we send it to the graph determined by declaring the first and
m
-th vertex of the initial graph(s) to coincide and which we number by
1
, then.
In the case the graph came from
B
, i.e. was connected, it is straightforward to number the remaining vertices of the resulting new graph.
In the case the graph comes from
C
we use the partition
L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=\{1,\cdots,m\}
which satisfied the condition that
1\in L_{1},m\in L_{2}
to mark the remaining vertices of the resulting new graph. To define the partition into two sets of the half-edges of the ‘new’ vertex of
\psi^{-1}(G,f)
we remember which ones came from the first vertex of
(G,f)
and which came from the
m
-th vertex of
(G,f)
.

Finally it is straightforward to check that these two assignments are inverse to each other.
∎

12.3.2
The Proof of Theorem K continued

12.3.14
allows us to prove following key technical theorem:

Theorem 12.3.16
.

For

66
66

66

Here
(-1)^{*}=\epsilon(i,j,1,..,\hat{i},..,\hat{j},..,n)

.

\displaystyle(-1)^{*}\mathcal{K}_{m-1}\big((x_{i}\cdot x_{j})\odot x_{1}\odot\ldots\hat{x_{i}}\ldots\hat{x_{j}}\ldots\odot x_{m}\big)

\displaystyle\gamma\mathcal{K}_{m}(x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot x_{m})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=[m]\\
i\in L_{1},j\in L_{2}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\#}\big(\mathcal{K}_{|L_{1}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}x_{i})\cdot\mathcal{K}_{|L_{2}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{2}}x_{i})\big)

Proof.

By definition we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{*}\mathcal{K}_{m-1}\big((x_{i}\cdot x_{j})\odot x_{1}\odot\ldots\hat{x_{i}}\ldots\hat{x_{j}}\ldots\odot x_{m}\big)

\displaystyle\sum_{g,n}\sum_{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m-1}}(-1)^{*}\frac{1}{|Aut(G,f)|}\cdot K_{(G,f)}\big((x_{i}\cdot x_{j}),x_{1},\ldots,\hat{x_{i}},\ldots,\hat{x_{j}},\ldots,x_{m}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle:E

We denote by

the operation of
contracting exactly once the letters of the symmetric words

\{x_{l}\}_{l=1,\cdots,m}\in\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})[6-2d]
with each other, using

. This gives the identification

\widetilde{ev}_{(G,f)}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})=\frac{1}{|Aut(G,f)|}\cdot K_{(G,f)}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}),

(12.3.17)

recalling definition
12.2.8
.
We can ask whether we can re-express in a similar way

(-1)^{*}\frac{1}{|Aut(G,f)|}\cdot K_{(G,f)}(x_{i}\cdot x_{j},x_{1},\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m}),

(12.3.18)

where

.
Indeed, again recalling the definition (

(I,J)\in P_{(G,f)}^{(k_{1},k_{2})}
and whose summands we suggestively denote by

(-1)^{*}\widetilde{ev}_{(G,f,I,J)}(x_{i},x_{j},x_{1},\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m}).

(12.3.19)

Remark 12.3.20
.

We will give a precise meaning to (
12.3.19
) in equations (
12.3.22
) and (
12.3.24
).

For the moment we repeat that

\displaystyle\sum_{I,J\in P_{(G,f)}}(-1)^{*}\widetilde{ev}_{(G,f,I,J)}

\displaystyle(x_{i},x_{j},x_{1}\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{*}\frac{1}{|Aut(G,f)|}\cdot K_{(G,f)}(x_{i}\cdot x_{j},x_{1}\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m})

Thus we can write

E=\sum_{g,n}\sum_{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m-1}}\sum_{I,J\in P_{(G,f)}}(-1)^{*}\widetilde{ev}_{(G,f,I,J)}(x_{i},x_{j},x_{1},\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m}).

(12.3.21)

Observation 12.3.22
.

We note that we can re-express the summands (
12.3.19
) of (
12.3.18
), applying the rule
12.2.8
, as

\displaystyle(-1)^{*}\widetilde{ev}_{(G,f,I,J)}

\displaystyle(x_{i},x_{j},x_{1},\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma\epsilon(i,1,..,\hat{i},..,\hat{j},..,n,j)\widetilde{ev}_{\psi(G,f,I,J)}(x_{i},x_{1}\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m},x_{j})

(12.3.23)

if
\psi(G,f,I,J)\in B_{(k_{1},k_{2})}^{g,n}
. Here the right hand side is defined in the standard way by rule
12.3.17
.
The additional
\gamma
factors in (
12.3.22
) arise from the fact that the appearing graphs on the right hand side have the same number of total edges, but one more vertex, thus decreasing the betti number by one as compared to the appearing graphs appearing on the left hand side (recalling definition
9.2.2
).

Similarly we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{*}\widetilde{ev}_{(G,f,I,J)}(x_{i},x_{j},x_{1},\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{ev}_{(G_{1},f_{1})}(\_,\cdots,\_)\cdot\widetilde{ev}_{(G_{2},f_{2})}(\_,\cdots,\_,)(-1)^{\sigma}(L_{1}\sqcup L_{2})_{*}(x_{i},x_{j},x_{1},\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m})

(12.3.24)

if
\psi(G,f,I,J)=:\Big((G_{1},f_{1}),(G_{2},f_{2}),L_{1},L_{2}\Big)\in C_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}
, recalling the notations from definition
12.3.11
.
In (
12.3.24
) we plug the
x_{l}
’s belonging to
L_{1}
, that is such that
l\in L_{1}
(among them
x_{i}
at the first place) into the first factor and the
x_{l}
’s belonging to
L_{2}
(among them
x_{j}
at the last place) into the second factor, indicated by
(L_{1}\sqcup L_{2})_{*}
.
The sign
(-1)^{\sigma}
arises from the resulting Koszul sign of the permutation of the
x_{l}
’s. There is no additional
\gamma
factors in (
12.3.24
) since on the right hand side we both increase the number of total vertices by one, but also the number of connected components from one to two, thus the total betti number stays the same.

Next we note that the indexing set of the second and third sum in (
12.3.21
) is in bijection with the set
A_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}
. Theorem
12.3.14
tells us that this set is in bijection with
B_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}\cup C_{k_{1},k_{2}}^{g,n}
and equations (
12.3.22
) and (
12.3.24
) imply that the evaluation maps are compatible with this bijection. Thus we have

\displaystyle E=

\displaystyle\sum_{g,n}\sum_{B^{g,n}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\epsilon(i,1,..,\hat{i},..,\hat{j},..,n,j)\widetilde{ev}_{(G,f)}(x_{i},x_{1}\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m},x_{j})

\displaystyle+\sum_{g,n}

\displaystyle\sum_{C^{g,n}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\widetilde{ev}_{(G_{1},f_{1})}(\_,,\cdots,\_)\cdot\widetilde{ev}_{(G_{2},f_{2})}(\_,,\cdots,\_)(-1)^{\sigma}(L_{I}\sqcup L_{J})_{*}(x_{i},x_{j},x_{1},\cdots,\hat{x_{i}},\cdots,\hat{x_{j}},\cdots,x_{m}).

Finally using the identification (
12.3.12
) and the fact that the evaluation maps are compatible with that identification we rewrite the first summand

\displaystyle E=

\displaystyle\gamma\sum_{g,n}\sum_{\widetilde{B}^{g,n}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\widetilde{ev}_{(G,f)}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{g,n}\sum_{\widetilde{C}^{g,n}_{k_{1},k_{2}}}\widetilde{ev}_{(G_{1},f_{1})}(\_,\cdots,\_)\cdot\widetilde{ev}_{(G_{2},f_{2})}(\_,\cdots,\_)(-1)^{\widetilde{\sigma}}(L_{1}\sqcup L_{2})_{*}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}).

and where we have rewritten the second summand using identification (
12.3.13
) and the fact that the evaluation maps are compatible with that. Now using again (
12.3.17
) we deduce that

\displaystyle E=\gamma\sum_{g,n}\sum_{(G,f)\in\widetilde{\Gamma(g,n)}_{m}}\frac{1}{|Aut(G,f)|}\cdot

\displaystyle K_{(G,f)}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})+

\displaystyle\sum_{g,n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+n_{2}=n\\
g_{1}+g_{2}=g\\
m_{1}+m_{2}=m\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G_{1}\in{\Gamma(g_{1},n_{1})}_{m_{1}}\\
G_{2}\in{\Gamma(g_{2},n_{2})}_{m_{2}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=[m]\\
i\in L_{1},j\in L_{2}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|Aut(G_{1})|\cdot|Aut(G_{2})|}

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{K}_{|L_{1}|}(\_,\cdots,\_)\cdot\mathcal{K}_{|L_{2}|}(\_,\cdots,\_)\big)(-1)^{\widetilde{\sigma}}(L_{1}\sqcup L_{2})_{*}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})

which by definition is equal to

=\gamma\mathcal{K}_{m}(x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot x_{m})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=[m]\\
i\in L_{1},j\in L_{2}\end{subarray}}\epsilon(L_{1},L_{2})\big(\mathcal{K}_{|L_{1}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}x_{i})\cdot\mathcal{K}_{|L_{2}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{2}}x_{i})\big),

which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Lemma 12.3.25
.

For all
m>1
and
(x_{1}\otimes y_{1})\odot\cdots\odot(x_{m}\otimes y_{m})\in Sym^{m}(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes W[6-2d])
we have
67
67

67

Here
(-1)^{*_{1}}=(-1)^{|y_{i}|}
and
(-1)^{*_{2}}=(-1)^{\sum_{i\in L_{1}}|y_{i}|}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=[m]\\
i\in L_{1},j\in L_{2}\end{subarray}}\big(\mathcal{K}_{|L_{1}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}x_{i})\cdot\mathcal{K}_{|L_{2}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{2}}x_{i})\big)\otimes(-1)^{*_{1}}\big(\{y_{i},y_{j}\}_{w}\cdot y_{1}\cdot\ldots\hat{y_{i}}\ldots\hat{y_{j}}\ldots\cdot y_{m}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=[m]\\
|L_{1}|,|L_{2}|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\big(\mathcal{K}_{|L_{1}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}x_{i})\cdot\mathcal{K}_{|L_{2}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{2}}x_{i})\big)\otimes(-1)^{*_{2}}\{\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}y_{i},\bigodot_{j\in L_{2}}y_{j}\}_{w}

Proof.

This follows directly from plugging the identity

(-1)^{\sum_{i\in L_{1}}|y_{i}|}\{\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}y_{i},\bigodot_{j\in L_{2}}y_{j}\}_{w}=\sum_{i\in L_{1},j\in L_{2}}(-1)^{|y_{i}|}\{y_{i},y_{j}\}_{w}\cdot y_{1}\cdot\ldots\hat{y_{i}}\ldots\hat{y_{j}}\ldots\cdot y_{m},

which follows from the Leibniz rule for
W
, into the second line, which gives the first line.
∎

Now we can easily give the proof of the key lemma
12.3.8
:

Proof.

For
m>1
and
(x_{1}\otimes y_{1})\odot\cdots\odot(x_{m}\otimes y_{m})\in Sym^{m}(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes W[6-2d])
we have
68
68

68

Here the signs
a
,
b
,
c
are as in the statement of lemma
12.3.8
. Further
(-1)^{d}=(-1)^{\sum_{i\in L_{1}}|y_{i}|}
times the Koszul sign of permuting the
x
’s to the left and
y
’s to right.

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}(-1)^{*_{a}}\mathcal{K}_{m-1}\big((x_{i}\cdot x_{j})\odot x_{1}\odot\ldots\hat{x_{i}}\ldots\hat{x_{j}}\ldots\odot x_{m}\big)\otimes\big(\{y_{i},y_{j}\}_{w}\cdot y_{1}\cdot\ldots\hat{y_{i}}\ldots\hat{y_{j}}\ldots\cdot y_{m}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}(-1)^{*_{b}}\gamma\mathcal{K}_{m}(x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot x_{m})\otimes\big(\{y_{i},y_{j}\}_{w}\cdot y_{1}\cdot\ldots\hat{y_{i}}\ldots\hat{y_{j}}\ldots\cdot y_{m}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=[m]\\
i\in L_{1},j\in L_{2}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{*_{d}}\big(\mathcal{K}_{|L_{1}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}x_{i})\cdot\mathcal{K}_{|L_{2}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{2}}x_{i})\big)\otimes\big(\{y_{i},y_{j}\}_{w}\cdot y_{1}\cdot\ldots\hat{y_{i}}\ldots\hat{y_{j}}\ldots\cdot y_{m}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}(-1)^{*_{b}}\gamma\mathcal{K}_{m}(x_{1}\odot\cdots\odot x_{m})\otimes\big(\{y_{i},y_{j}\}_{w}\cdot y_{1}\cdot\ldots\hat{y_{i}}\ldots\hat{y_{j}}\ldots\cdot y_{m}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}=[m]\\
|L_{1}|,|L_{2}|\geq 1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{*_{c}}\big(\mathcal{K}_{|L_{1}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}x_{i})\cdot\mathcal{K}_{|L_{2}|}(\bigodot_{i\in L_{2}}x_{i})\big)\otimes\{\bigodot_{i\in L_{1}}y_{i},\bigodot_{j\in L_{2}}y_{j}\}_{w}

Here the first equality follows from theorem
12.3.16
(to be more precise from multiplying the equality stated by
(-1)^{|y_{i}|}
times the Koszul sign from permuting in
(x_{1}\otimes y_{1})\odot\cdots\odot(x_{m}\otimes y_{m})
the
x
’s to the left and
y
’s to right ). The second equality follows from lemma
12.3.25
(again multiplied by that same Koszul sign).
∎

With this we have now proven theorem
12.3.4
; let us quickly recall why:

Summary 12.3.26
.

As explained (beneath its statement) proving theorem
12.3.4
is equivalent to proving equation
12.3
. By equations
12.3
and
12.3
proving equation
12.3
is equivalent to proving lemma
12.3.8
. Finally theorem
12.3.16
and the easy lemma
12.3.25
together give a proof of lemma
12.3.8
, as we just explained above.

A direct corollary is:

Corollary 12.3.27
.

Given a dimension
d
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, a splitting
s
of the non-commutative Hodge filtration and
\Lambda,
a full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. Then there is an
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism

\mathcal{K}_{s}\otimes m:\ \mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\Lambda)[5-2d]\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\Lambda)[5-2d].

Proof.

We apply theorem
12.3.4
to the
(2d-5)
-twisted BD algebra
\mathcal{F}^{o}(\Lambda)
from definition
12.1.1
.
∎

Given a dimension
d
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and a splitting
s
there is an isomorphism of induced dg Lie algebras (recalling definitions
5.1.3
and

)

R:\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{triv}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv},

(12.3.28)

see beginning of section 9.1 of

]
, keeping in mind that we denoted eg.
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathcal{C}}^{triv}=\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{triv}.
Thus it follows:

Corollary 12.3.29
.

Given a dimension
d
cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, a splitting
s
of the non-commutative Hodge filtration and
\Lambda,
a full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. Then there is an
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism

\Psi_{s}^{oc}:\ \mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\Lambda)[5-2d]\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\Lambda)[5-2d],

given by composing the isomorphism (
12.3.28
) with the
L_{\infty}
-map from corollary

.

Thus we have proven theorem K from the introduction.

13
Twisted Holography from Calabi-Yau Categories

In this section we elaborate on the circle of ideas presented in the introduction, in section
1.3.3
, on how to formulate a version of twisted holography at the level of partition function, given as input datum a Calabi-Yau categories and an object of that category. First, we comment on further fundamental aspects of the open-closed story, citing partly the forthcoming work
[
4
]
. In particular we explain how assumption
(*)
from the introduction should be satisfied in general, using the existence of a Maurer-Cartan element in the universal open-closed BD algebra.

These ideas are still in preparation and we should warn the reader that for instance details regarding shifts (see remarks
5.1.2
and
12.1.2
) still need to be pinned-down.

13.1
Fundamental Open-Closed Structures

Our setting in this section is as follows: Let
\mathcal{C}
be a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and
\Lambda\subseteq\mathcal{C}
some choice of objects. As explained eg. in definition
12.1.3
there is an associated tensor product BD algebra

\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\Lambda).

Given such a datum we have seen in theorem
1.2.7
and theorem B that there are maps of induced
L_{\infty}
-algebras

\rho_{\mathcal{C}}^{c}:M^{c}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})

(13.1.1)

respectively

\rho_{{\Lambda}}^{o}:M^{o}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{o}(\Lambda)

(13.1.2)

where
M^{c}
respectively
M^{o}_{\Lambda}
are built from the moduli space of ‘closed’ Riemann surfaces respectively ‘open’ Riemann surfaces (the latter colored by
\Lambda
). There is an open-closed analogue of these BD algebras: From now on we take for simplicity
\Lambda=\{\lambda\}
. The chain complex

\Big(\bigoplus_{stable}C_{*}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n,b,m_{1},\cdots,m_{b}}^{fr,})_{hS^{1}},\partial\Big)

(13.1.3)

computes the (
S^{1}
-equivariant) cohomology of the moduli space of genus
g
Riemann surfaces with
n
framed boundaries and
b
unframed boundaries each with
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
marked points, the free boundaries decorated by
\lambda
; we refer to
[
62
]
for details on this and for the following. One can define a BD algebra structure

M^{oc}:=\Big(\bigoplus_{stable}C_{*}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n,b,m_{1},\cdots,m_{b}}^{fr})_{hS^{1}}\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket,\partial+\gamma\Delta_{c}+\gamma\delta+\nabla,\{\_,\_\}_{c}+\{\_,\_\}_{o}\Big)

(13.1.4)

on the chain complex (
13.1.3
), where
\{\_,\_\}_{o}
,
\delta
and
\nabla
are induced by gluing boundary marked points (
\delta
gluing together marked points lying on different boundary components and
\nabla
those which lie on the same boundary component), further
\{\_,\_\}_{c}
and
\Delta_{c}
twist-glue interior marked points.
Following theorem in writing generalizes the maps (
13.1.1
) and (
13.1.2
) to the open-closed setting:

Theorem 13.1.5

(
[
4
]
)
.

Given
\mathcal{C}
a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
there is an open-closed representing map of induced
L_{\infty}
-algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{oc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{C},\lambda}}

Remark 13.1.6
.

Theorem
13.1.5
is still in preparation and the author is not aware how Amorim and Tu will set up exactly the gradings for
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)
. Recall remarks
5.1.2
and
12.1.2
for different set-ups used in this thesis. We suppress this point in the following, but ask the careful reader to keep this in mind.

In the purely closed BD algebra we could find a canonical Maurer-Cartan element, the closed string vertices from equation (
1.2.9
). In the purely open BD algebra this was not the case. We did study related phenomena, however (see eg. theorem G.1 from the introduction).

To explain the analogous statement for the open-closed set-up we need to introduce an additional map.
Denote by

\Delta_{co}:M^{oc}\rightarrow M^{oc}

(13.1.7)

the degree 1 differential induced from declaring a framed boundary to be a non-framed boundary with zero marked points (see
[
62
]
, just before theorem 4.1). Now we can state

Theorem 13.1.8

(
[
99
]
,
[
62
]
)
.

There is an element
SV^{oc}\in M^{oc}
, called
open-closed string vertices
, playing the role of a fundamental class. It is
almost
a Maurer-Cartan element, up the the term coming from
\Delta_{co}
, that is it

(\partial+\gamma\Delta_{c}+\gamma\delta+\nabla)SV^{oc}+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\{SV^{oc},SV^{oc}\}_{o}+\{SV^{oc},SV^{oc}\}_{c}\Big)+\Delta_{co}SV^{oc}=0.

(13.1.9)

Under the map
\rho_{\mathcal{C},\lambda}
the differential
\Delta_{co}
has an interesting categorical meaning, which will appear in the forthcoming work
[
4
]
. Denote by
ch(\lambda)\in CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\llbracket u\rrbracket
the non-commutative Chern character of
\lambda
induced by the class of the unit of the unital algebra
End_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda)
. Further denote

\langle ch(\lambda),\_,\rangle_{res}:\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})

the degree 1 map extending as a derivation the pairing with
ch(\lambda)
via the higher residue pairing
1.2.2
which we weight by
\nu
, the basis of cyclic words of length zero:

Lemma 13.1.10

(
[
4
]
)
.

Given
\mathcal{C}
a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
then the differential (
13.1.7
) intertwines the map (
13.1.5
) as follows

\rho_{\mathcal{C},\lambda}\circ\Delta_{co}=(\nu\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res})\circ\rho.

Note that lemma
13.1.10
together with theorem
13.1.5
explain that assumption
(*)
from the introduction is satisfied in general:

Corollary 13.1.11

(
[
4
]
)
.

Given a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and an object
{\lambda\in\mathcal{C}}
there is an element
S^{oc}\in\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)
given as

S^{oc}:=\rho_{\mathcal{C},\Lambda}(SV^{oc})

such that it is a Maurer-Cartan element up to a term coming from the Chern character

(d_{hoch}+uB+\gamma\Delta_{c}+d+\gamma\delta+\nabla)S^{oc}+\frac{1}{2}\{S^{oc},S^{oc}\}_{o}+\frac{1}{2}\{S^{oc},S^{oc}\}_{c}+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),S^{oc}\rangle_{res}=0

(13.1.12)

and its open tree level part
69
69

69

That is the projection to
Sym^{0}(\cdots)\otimes Sym^{1}(Cyc^{*}(\lambda)[-1])\gamma^{0}
.
is the hamiltonian of the
A_{\infty}
-algebra, see eg. (
1.3.28
).

The second property should follow from the fact that the map
\rho_{\mathcal{C},\Lambda}
when restricted to the purely open part coincides with the map
13.1.1
and that the purely open, genus zero and one boundary component part of the open-closed string vertices is described by the disk Maurer-Cartan element from theorem
9.1.22
, which gets mapped to the hamiltonian of the cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra as described in lemma
10.1.21
.

13.2
Quantization of Open String Field Theory

In this section we explain when we can find a quantization (in the sense of definition
1.3.17
) of the (large
N
) open string field theory of an objects
\lambda
(or better the objects
\lambda^{\oplus N}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, assuming that
\mathcal{C}
is additive) using the theory laid out in the previous section
13.1
and corollary
12.3.29
. As we will see, this quantization depends on a splitting of the non-commutative Hodge filtration
4.5.1
and a trivialization of the non-commutative Chern character.

Dependent on theorem
13.1.5
and lemma
13.1.10
from
[
4
]
in preparation we have:

Proposition 13.2.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
be a tuple of a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and an object
\lambda
of
\mathcal{C}
. Assume that
(d_{hoch}+uB)h=ch(\lambda)
, a trivialization of the Chern character of
\lambda
. Then

S^{oc,q}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h):=e^{(\nu\langle h,\_\rangle_{res})}\rho(SV^{oc})

(13.2.2)

is a Maurer-Cartan element of
{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda).
Further if we change
h
by an exact term
S^{oc,q}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)
changes up to homotopy.

Proof.

Let us denote for simplicity by
d_{oc}
the full BD differential of
{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)
and by
\{\_,\_\}_{oc}
its shifted Poisson bracket. The proposition follows by computing

\displaystyle d_{oc}\Big(e^{\nu\langle h,-\rangle_{res}}S^{oc}\Big)=e^{\nu\langle h,-\rangle_{res}}d_{oc}S^{oc}+e^{\nu\langle h,-\rangle_{res}}\nu\langle ch(\lambda),-\rangle_{res}S^{oc}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big(\Delta_{oc}I^{oc}+\frac{1}{2}\{I^{oc},I^{oc}\}_{oc}+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),e^{\nu\langle h,-\rangle_{res}}S^{oc}\rangle_{res}\Big)+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),-\rangle_{res}e^{\nu\langle h,-\rangle_{res}}S^{oc}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\big(\Delta_{oc}I^{oc}+\frac{1}{2}\{I^{oc},I^{oc}\}_{oc}\big),

where the first equality uses lemma 6.0.3 (3) of
[
38
]
and for the second equality we used corollary
13.1.11
and that
e^{\nu\langle h,-\rangle_{res}}
commutes with the brackets and BV differentials. Similarly we can compute that

(d_{oc}+d_{t})\Big(e^{\nu\langle h+tdg+dtg,-\rangle_{res}}S^{oc}\Big)\in MCE\Big({\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)\otimes\Omega^{*}([0,1])\Big),

where
d_{t}
denotes the de Rham differential on
\Omega^{*}([0,1])
. This explains the independence up to homotopy if we change
I^{oc}(\lambda,h)
by an exact terms.
∎

We collect some ingredients to prove theorem
13.2.7
below: We recall that corollary
12.3.29
tells us that given a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, an object
\lambda
and a splitting s of the non-commutative Hodge filtration there is a map of
L_{\infty}
-algebras

\Psi^{oc}_{s}:\ \mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda).

Thus from proposition
13.2.1
, given additionally a trivialization
(d_{hoch}+uB)h=ch(\lambda)
of the Chern character, and corollary
12.3.29
it follows that

\Psi^{oc}_{s}(S^{oc,q}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h))\in MCE\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)\Big).

(13.2.3)

Let us introduce following definition in order to reduce the notational clutter a bit.

Definition 13.2.4
.

We call a
package

\mathcal{P}:=\{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s\}

(13.2.5)

a tuple consisting of a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
, an object in that category
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
, a trivialization
(d_{hoch}+uB)h=ch(\lambda)
and a splitting
s
.

Let us denote by

\pi:\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)\rightarrow Sym^{0}(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\llbracket u\rrbracket)\gamma^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)\cong\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)

the above composite, which is a map of BD algebras. Given a package
\mathcal{P}
we denote

S^{o,q}(\mathcal{P}):=(\pi\circ\Psi^{oc}_{s})\big(S^{oc,q}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)\big)\in\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda),

(13.2.6)

recalling definition
13.2.2
.
A consequence of the fact that
\pi
is a map of BD algebras, equation (
13.2.3
), the explicit form of the map
\Psi^{oc}_{s}
and the second part of corollary
13.1.11
is:

Theorem 13.2.7
.

Given a package
\mathcal{P}
as in (
13.2.5
) the element

S^{o,q}(\mathcal{P})
from (
13.2.6
) is a quantization of the
A_{\infty}
-hamiltonian of the cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra
End_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda)
, that is it is a solution to the quantum master equation in
\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)
and its tree level component is the hamiltonian of the cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebra
End_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda)
.

Definition 13.2.8
.

Given a package
\mathcal{P}
we denote the
partition function of the large
N
open string field theory
by

Z^{o}(\mathcal{P}):=[e^{S^{o,q}(\mathcal{P})}]\in H_{*}\big(\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda),d+\gamma\delta+\nabla\big).

(13.2.9)

To justify this name we recall the quantized LQT map, which is a map of BV algebras
[
54
]

LQT_{N}:\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)\rightarrow C^{*}\big(\mathfrak{gl}_{N}End_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda)\big).

(13.2.10)

The right-hand side of (
13.2.10
) describes the observables of the
open string gauge theory on a stack of
N
branes of
\lambda
modeled by the cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra

End_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda^{\oplus N})\cong\mathfrak{gl}_{N}End_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda),

(13.2.11)

remarking that above isomorphism
13.2.11
of course is only applicable in additive categories. Summarizing, theorem
13.2.7
tell us when to find a quantization of this
L_{\infty}
-algebra, and uniformly for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
. See also the discussion around equation (
1.3.32
) from the introduction, which justifies the name of
Z^{o}(\mathcal{P})
as the partition function of the quantized large
N
open string field theory on a stack of
N
branes of type
\lambda
, associated to a package
\mathcal{P}
(
1.3.31
).

Remark 13.2.12
.

Another interesting element to consider should be the following. Given a package
\mathcal{P}

this element is defined as

e^{\Psi^{oc}_{s}(S^{oc,q}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h))/\gamma}\in{\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}^{Triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda),

recalling equation (
13.2.3
).
From this equation it follows that

Z^{oc}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s}:=[e^{\Psi^{oc}_{s}(S^{oc,q}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h))/\gamma}]\in H_{*}\Big({\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}^{Triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda),d_{Hoch}+d+\gamma\delta+\nabla\Big),

which may justify calling
Z^{oc}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s}
the open-closed GW potential. We don’t consider this element in this thesis, however.

13.3
Backreacted Closed String Field Theory

In physics, there is the idea that a closed string field theory, which we introduced in section
5
, can be deformed by introducing terms sourced by branes (but no other open strings). In the universal geometric open-closed BV algebra this picture of a brane
\lambda
sourcing closed strings should be encoded by those surfaces (possibly with framed boundaries) which additionally have some (unframed) boundaries with no marked points which are colored by an element
\lambda
. We can ask whether the corresponding subset of the open-closed string vertices, which we denote by
SV|^{sourced}_{closed}
, still satisfies the quantum master equation. Upon examination of the quantum master equation (
13.1.9
) we note that this is not true. Also for the classical part of (
13.1.9
), which we denote by
SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}
, this is not true. However, we can ask that their image under the open-closed representing map from theorem
13.1.5
satisfies the master equation, leading us to the following:

13.3.1
Classical Backreacted Closed String Field Theory

We try to overcome the problem of
SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\in M^{oc}
not satisfying the classical master equation by making a definition (which we hope to improve, see remark
13.3.20
later).

Definition 13.3.1
.

We say that a pair of a smooth cyclic category
\mathcal{C}
and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
are
backreactable
if, recalling the open-closed representing map
\rho_{\mathcal{C},\lambda}
of theorem
13.1.5
, we have that

\rho_{\mathcal{C},\lambda}\big(\{SV^{oc}|^{m=1}_{g=0},SV^{oc}|^{m=1}_{g=0}\}_{c}\big)=0

(13.3.2)

and

\rho_{\mathcal{C},\lambda}\big(\nabla SV^{oc}|^{m=2}_{g=0}\big)=0,

(13.3.3)

referring to this footnote
70
70

70

Here we denote by
SV^{oc}|^{m=1}_{g=0}
the genus zero part of the string vertices with any number of framed boundaries and unframed boundaries, of which exactly one unframed boundaries has one marked point. Similarly we denote by
SV^{oc}|^{m=2}_{g=0}
the genus zero part of the string vertices with any number of framed boundaries and unframed boundaries, of which exactly one unframed boundaries has two marked point.
for the notation.

In this section we define ‘classical sourced closed string field theory’ (see definition
13.3.9
) and the ‘classical backreacted closed string field theory’ (see definition
13.3.15
) in order to shed light on the backreaction phenomena. In short, given a category and an object that are backreactable we can always define the former, while the latter only exists additionally assuming a trivialization of the Chern character of the object. However, given such a trivialization, we can actually identify both structures, see remark
13.3.16
. We further examine carefully the curvature of the underlying
L_{\infty}
-algebras defining these classical field theories.

A central element for these definitions is the element
SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}
, the genus zero open-closed string vertices with arbitrary number of framed and unframed boundaries but no marked points; compare equation (
13.1.9
). Assuming that
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
is backreactable it follows from the definition and corollary
13.1.11
that

(d_{Hoch}+uB+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res})\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\{\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big),\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)\}_{c}\Big)=0,

(13.3.4)

which implies that the derivation

d_{Hoch}+uB+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res}+\{\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big),\_\}_{c}

(13.3.5)

defines a differential on
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu]
. By setting
\nu=N
the induced differential on the algebra

\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}=Sym(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}])

dually induces a dg coalgebra structure on the graded vector space

Sym\big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]\big)^{\vee},

that is we recover a possibly
curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra
structure on the graded vector space
\big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]\big)^{\vee}
. This is the first piece of data allowing us to define the notion of the ‘classical sourced closed string field theory at level
N
’ (see definition
13.3.9
below):

Definition 13.3.6
.

Given
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
that are backreactable we denote by
L_{N}(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
the curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra on cyclic cochains of
\mathcal{C}
induced from the differential (
13.3.5
) and by setting
\nu=N
.

Lemma 13.3.7
.

Given
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
that are backreactable then the curvature of
L_{N}(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
is given to leading order in
N
by

N\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res}\in\big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]\big)^{\vee}.

(13.3.8)

Proof.

The fact that

N\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res}\in\big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]\big)^{\vee}

contributes to the leading order in
N
of the curvature is clear. The projection of
\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)
onto
Sym^{1}(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}])[\nu]
is in general non-zero and these terms do also contribute to the curving of
L_{N}(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
under the differential induced from

\{\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed},\_\}_{c}.

However, because of the stability condition on the open-closed string vertices (see around equation 1 of
[
62
]
) the sub-collection of the open-closed string vertices which have
n=1
framed boundaries need to have
b\geq 2
unframed boundaries in the genus zero case. Thus the image of the projection of
\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)
onto
Sym^{1}(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}])[\nu]
actually lands in the part with higher or equal to 2 powers of
\nu
; thus the contribution to the curvature coming from
\{\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed},\_\}_{c}
is of higher order in
N
.
∎

By definition
13.3.1
if
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
is backreactable we have that

\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)\in MCE\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu],d_{Hoch}+uB+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big)

and by setting
\nu=N
we get

\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)_{N}\in MCE\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0},d_{Hoch}+uB+N\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big).

Thus we can twist by
\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)_{N}
leading to following definition, which we contrast with definition
5.2.2
of classical interacting closed string field theory:

Definition 13.3.9
.

Given a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
together with
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
that is backreactable we call
classical sourced closed string field theory at level
N
the datum of the shifted Poisson algebra

\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0},\cdot,d_{Hoch}+uB+N\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res}+\{\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)_{N},\_\}_{c},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big).

and the curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L_{N}(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
from definition
13.3.6
, whose curvature (to leading order in
N
) is given by the Chern character; see equation (
13.3.8
).

Remark 13.3.10
.

In the setting of the preceding definition we can define a shifted Poisson algebra at level
N=\infty

\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu],\cdot,d_{Hoch}+uB+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res}+\{\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big),\_\}_{c},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big),

but it is not clear to us what is the good analogue of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on cyclic cochains.

Now we begin to collect some ideas to define the ’classical backreacted closed string field theory’, see definition
13.3.15
below. For that we assume that we are given a trivialization of the Chern character of an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
of a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category, which gives us:

Lemma 13.3.11
.

Given
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
together with a trivialization
ch(\lambda)=(d_{hoch}+uB)h
then

e^{\nu\langle h,\_\rangle_{res}}:\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu],\cdot,d_{Hoch}+uB+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big)\rightarrow\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu],\cdot,d_{Hoch}+uB,\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big)

(13.3.12)

defines an isomorphism of shifted Poisson algebras with inverse given by the map

e^{-\nu\langle h,\_\rangle_{res}}:\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu]\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu].

Proof.

This follows similarly to the proof of proposition
13.2.2
.
∎

Let us now additionally assume that
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
is backreactable. Since the element
\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)
is then a Maurer-Cartan element of the left hand side of map
13.3.12
by lemma
13.3.11
its image is also a Maurer-Cartan element. From this it follows that the derivation

d_{Hoch}+uB+\{e^{\nu\langle h,\_\rangle_{res}}\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big),\_\}_{c}:\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu]\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0}[\nu]

(13.3.13)

squares to zero. In the same way as in the discussion around equation
13.3.5
we conclude that by setting
\nu=N
the differential (
13.3.13
) defines an
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on cyclic cochains:

Definition 13.3.14
.

Given
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
which are backreactable together with a trivialization
ch(\lambda)=(d_{hoch}+uB)h
we denote by
L_{N}^{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)
the
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on the graded vector space
\big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]\big)^{\vee}
induced from the differential (
13.3.13
) by setting
\nu=N
and by dualizing.

Lemma
13.3.11
and the preceding discussion imply that following definition is well-defined:

Definition 13.3.15
.

Given
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
that are backreactable together with a trivialization
ch(\lambda)=(d_{hoch}+uB)h
. We call

classical closed string field theory at level
N
backreacted in the direction
(\lambda,h)
the datum of the shifted Poisson algebra

\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|_{\gamma=0},\cdot,d_{Hoch}+uB+\{e^{\nu\langle h,\_\rangle_{res}}\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)_{N},\_\}_{c},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big).

and the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L_{N}^{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)
from definition
13.3.14
.

Remark 13.3.16
.

The map
13.3.11
induces an
isomorphism from ‘the classical sourced closed string field theory at level
N
’ (from definition
13.3.9
) to ‘the classical closed string field theory at level
N
backreacted in the direction
(\lambda,h)
’ from definition
13.3.15
; that is we have an isomorphism of the shifted Poisson algebras featuring in these definitions (which follows by twisting the map
13.3.11
with the Maurer-Cartan element
\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)
) and an isomorphism of
L_{\infty}
-algebras

L_{N}(\mathcal{C},\lambda)\rightsquigarrow L_{N}^{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h),

(which follows since the map from lemma
13.3.11
twisted by the Maurer-Cartan element
\rho\big(SV_{g=0}|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)
is still a map of algebras, inducing a map of coalgebras by dualizing). The
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L_{N}^{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)
is possibly curved, however its curving is of higher than first order in
N
; this follows by arguments as in lemma
13.3.7
.
As an upshot,
this tells us that if
ch(\lambda)=(d_{hoch}+uB)h
the curving (
13.3.8
) of
L_{N}(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
is in fact trivial to leading order in
N
(up to
L_{\infty}
isomorphism).

Remark 13.3.17
.

Assuming additionally the existence of a splitting
s
the loop zero part of the map from corollary
12.3.29
induces (as it is a map of algebras, which follows from the
\gamma=0
part of theorem
12.3.16
) an
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism

L_{N}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)\rightsquigarrow\Big(\big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]\big)^{\vee},d_{hoch}+uB\Big),

that is it implies, combining this result with the previous remark, that (up to
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism) the
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure
L_{N}(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
is abelian and in particular that its curving (
13.3.8
) is (up to
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism) trivial.

Recall also the discussion in the introduction in remark
1.3.40
about backreaction for BCOV theory on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
for a conjectural (almost) example of these considerations.

13.3.2
Quantization of Backreacted Closed String Field Theory

Next we introduce a condition that will allow us to find a quantization of the classical closed backreacted string field theory. Furthermore we phrase ideas for what its partition function encodes via conjecture
13.3.32
below.

Definition 13.3.18
.

We say that a pair
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
of a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
is
holographic
if it is backreactable (recalling definition
13.3.1
) and in addition

\delta\rho(SV^{oc}|_{g=0}^{m=1+1})=0,

(13.3.19)

referring to this footnote
71
71

71

Here we denote by
SV^{oc}|_{g=0}^{m=1+1}
the part of the genus zero open-closed string vertices with arbitrarily many framed boundaries and arbitrarily many unframed boundaries out of which exactly two have one marked point each.
for the notation.

Remark 13.3.20
.

It seems likely that a tuple of a dimension
d
Calabi-Yau category
\mathcal{C}
and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
are both ‘backreactable’ and ‘holographic’ if
End_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda)
is isomorphic to the
CW
complex of a
d
-sphere. This is a condition that has been used in the geometric contexts of open Gromov-Witten theory, see

[
46
]
.

Roughly speaking, in our set-up this should follow as one can use models for cyclic cohomology where one quotients out by the units; compare section 2.2.13 and 2.2.14 of
[
76
]
. However if we can ’set the units to zero’ and work under the assumption that
End_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda)
is spanned by the unit and its dual (which is the case for the
CW
complex of a
d
-sphere), then one can reasonably expect that the terms (
13.3.2
), (
13.3.3
) and (
13.3.19
) are zero.
To be a bit more precise, one may need to relax conditions (
13.3.2
), (
13.3.3
) and (
13.3.19
) suitably for the above argument to work, likely in the sense that the featuring terms are exact but not necessarily zero. But then proving an analogue of equations (
13.3.4
) and (
13.3.23
) seems more difficult, which we left for the future.

In order to find in theorem
13.3.24
below a quantization of classical closed string field theory backreacted in the direction
(\lambda,h)
we need following higher genus version of lemma
13.3.11
.

Lemma 13.3.21
.

Given
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
together with a trivialization
ch(\lambda)=(d_{hoch}+uB)h
then

\hskip-14.22636pte^{\nu\langle h,\_\rangle_{res}}:\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|[\nu],\cdot,d_{Hoch}+uB+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res}+\gamma\Delta_{c},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big)\rightarrow\Big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})|[\nu],\cdot,d_{Hoch}+uB+\gamma\Delta_{c},\{\_,\_\}_{c}\Big)

(13.3.22)

defines an isomorphism of BD algebras.

Proof.

This follows similarly to the proof of proposition
13.2.2
.
∎

Denote by
SV|^{sourced}_{closed}
the (all genus) open-closed string vertices with arbitrary number of framed and unframed boundaries but no marked points; compare equation (
13.1.9
). Assuming that
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
is holographic a direct consequence is then that

(d_{Hoch}+uB+\nu\langle ch(\lambda),\_\rangle_{res}+\gamma\Delta_{c})\rho\big(SV|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\{\rho\big(SV|^{sourced}_{closed}\big),\rho\big(SV|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)\}_{c}=0,

(13.3.23)

that is it is a Maurer-Cartan element in the left hand side of map (
13.3.22
); thus its image under that map is as well.

Now we can argue that the element (
13.3.25
) below defines a
quantization of the classical closed string field theory backreacted in the direction
(\lambda,h)
:

Theorem 13.3.24
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
be holographic and pick a trivialization of the Chern character
(d_{hoch}+uB)h=ch(\lambda)
. Then

S^{c,q}_{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h):=e^{\nu\langle h,\_\rangle_{res}}\rho\big(SV|^{sourced}_{closed}\big)

(13.3.25)

is a Maurer-Cartan element of
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})[\nu]

and its genus zero part induces via

\{S^{c,q}_{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)|_{\gamma=0},\_\}

the square zero odd vector field defining the
L_{\infty}
-algebra of classical closed string field theory backreacted in the direction
(\lambda,h)
from definition
13.3.15
.

Remark 13.3.26
.

Theorem
13.3.24
follows easily from corollary
13.1.11
and the definition of being holographic. However, one may hope to generalize what it means to be holographic in the sense of definition
13.3.18
, like asking that the term in equation
13.3.19
is exact and not directly zero. It seems that a proof of the analog of theorem
13.3.24
in this situation is more complicated, which we will study in the future.

To explain definition
13.3.29
further below we now additionally assume that we are given a splitting
s
of the non-commutative Hodge filtration, that is all together a package

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s}=\{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s\}

(13.3.27)

of a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
which are holographic, a trivialization
{(d_{hoch}+uB)h=ch(\lambda)}
and a splitting
s
. Then, recalling the map
\Psi_{s}^{oc}
from corollary
12.3.29
it follows that

\Psi_{s}^{oc}S^{c,q}_{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)\in H_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\llbracket\nu\rrbracket)

as is its exponential

e^{\Psi_{s}^{oc}S^{c,q}_{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)/\gamma}\in H_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\llbracket\nu\rrbracket).

(13.3.28)

Let us recall the element

Z_{\mathcal{C},s}^{c}\in H_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}

from definition
1.2.13
, which also coincides the summand of the element from (
13.3.28
) that has zero powers of
\nu
. Then we make following definition

Definition 13.3.29
.

Given a package

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s}=\{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s\}

of a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
which are holographic, a trivialization
{(d_{hoch}+uB)h=ch(\lambda)}
and a splitting
s
. Then denote the
partition function of the backreacted closed string field theory associated to
\mathcal{C}
in the direction of
\lambda
associated to the package
\mathcal{P}

Z^{c,br}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s}):=[e^{\Psi_{s}^{oc}S^{c,q}_{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)/\gamma}]-Z_{\mathcal{C},s}^{c}\in H_{*}\big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\llbracket\nu\rrbracket\big).

(13.3.30)

Remark 13.3.31
.

In definition
13.3.29
we made the ad-hoc choice of subtracting the term
{Z_{\mathcal{C},s}^{c}\in H_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}}

from definition
1.2.13
. We have no direct mathematical or physical motivation for doing so. However, because of this definition the element
Z^{c,br}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s})\in H_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}
lives in the subspace of strictly positive powers of
\nu
. This is a necessary condition for conjecture
13.4.1
below to hold, which is the reason we made that choice in definition
13.3.29
. We hope to better understand this ad-hoc choice in the future.

Recall that

H_{*}\big(\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\llbracket\nu\rrbracket\big)=Sym(HH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}])\llbracket\nu\rrbracket,

thus we can read off the coefficients (as a power series in
\nu
) of the element (
13.3.30
) with respect to homology class insertions and powers of
u
, as explained around equation (
1.1.1
) in the introduction.
What do these coefficients describe?

Conjecture 13.3.32
.

[Backreacted Calabi-Yau Category]
Given a holography package
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s}
as in definition
13.3.29
there is a family of Calabi-Yau categories
\mathcal{C}^{br}_{N}
, depending on
N\in\mathbb{N}
, together with a splitting
s^{br}
such that

HH_{*}(\mathcal{C})\cong HH_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{br}_{N})

(13.3.33)

and

{Z_{\mathcal{C}^{br}_{N},s^{br}}^{c}}=Z^{c,br}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s})|_{\nu=N}\in Sym(HH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]).

(13.3.34)

Here on the left hand side we refer to definition
1.2.13
applied to the conjectural Calabi-Yau category
\mathcal{C}^{br}_{N}
and the splitting
s^{br},
whereas on the right we refer to element (
13.3.30
) evaluated at
\nu=N
; further we need to assume equation (
13.3.33
) to make sense of equation (
13.3.34
).

Let us explain the motivation for conjecture
13.3.32
. The genus zero part of the element (
13.3.30
), more precisely the induced
L_{\infty}
-algebra(s)
L_{N}^{br}(\mathcal{C},\lambda,h)
from definition
13.3.15
should describes some deformed geometry, as compared to the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L(\mathcal{C})
described around equation (
5.2.1
).

For instance
72
72

72

Note that we cannot apply our methods to the following setting as
\mathbb{C}^{3}
is not proper Calabi-Yau; so the following can only serve for motivational purposes.
taking
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}^{b}_{dg}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
, its object(s)
\lambda^{\oplus N}
determined by the stack of
N
branes of
\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
h
given by the Mauer-Cartan element described in lemma 1 of
[
29
]
, Costello-Gaiotto find a dg-Lie algebra (for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
) describing the (deformations of the complex structure of the) deformed conifold with period
N
denoted
X_{N}
, which is obtained by perturbing the dg-Lie algebra describing the (deformations of the complex structure of)
\mathbb{C}^{3}
in ‘the direction’ of
\lambda^{\oplus N}
and using
h
(section 4 and 4.1 of
[
29
]
); see also the discussion in the introduction in remark
1.3.40
. However the deformed conifold with period
N
is again a Calabi-Yau manifold with its associated Calabi-Yau category
\mathcal{D}^{b}_{dg}(X_{N})
. Conjecture
13.3.32
postulates in particular that such a phenomena should hold in general and at a purely categorical level.

In a similar vein Gopakumar-Vafa
[
55
]
describe how the cotangent bundle of a three manifold (to which we can assign the Calabi-Yau category of the Fukaya category of the cotangent bundle) gets deformed in the presence of the zero section (which is an object of the Fukaya category) to the resolved conifold. This is again a Calabi-Yau manifold with associated Calabi-Yau category of quasi-coherent sheaves (also here a subtle
N
-dependence is present).

Questions around conjecture
13.3.32
have also recently been considered in the physics literature; see
[
48
]
, especially section 11.

13.4
Holography from a Calabi Yau Category
\mathcal{C}
and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
at the Level of Partition Functions

In physics, the so called top-down approach to holography proposes a duality between the large
N
open string field theory on a stack of
N
branes of a string theory and the backreacted closed string field theory in the direction of that same brane
[
79
]
,
[
29
]
. This should in particular imply that the partition functions of the two theories can be identified. This and the discussion in the previous sections leads us to formulate:

Conjecture 13.4.1

(Conjecture H)
.

Given a package
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s}=\{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s\}

of a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and an object
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}
which are holographic, a trivialization
{(d_{hoch}+uB)h=ch(\lambda)}
and a splitting
s
. Then
the partition function of the backreacted closed SFT (
13.3.30
) is homologous to the partition function of large
N
open SFT (
13.2.9
). That is

\iota_{c}(Z^{c,br}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s}))\simeq\iota_{o}(Z^{o}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,h,s})),

as elements of
H_{*}\Big({\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}^{triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda),d_{Hoch}+d+\Delta_{o}\Big)

under the natural inclusion maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})^{Triv}\llbracket\nu\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{o}(\lambda).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{o}}

We refer to the last part of the introduction, section
1.3.3
, for further motivation and conjectural examples of conjecture H. There we explain its relevance in trying to give a new and mathematical perspective on known and proposed relations
[
67
,
55
,
29
]
between large
N
gauge theories and enumerative geometry.

Remark 13.4.2
.

In physics, both sides of the conjecture, the partition function of large
N
open SFT and the partition function of closed backreacted SFT depend on certain parameters, identified under holography. Already for the (non-backreacted) GW potential (
1.2.13
) one expects a dependence on such parameters. We imagine that in the categorical approach this could be encoded as follows:

Theorem
13.4.4
below implies that given a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and taking
\Lambda\subseteq\mathcal{C}
to be
\Lambda=\mathcal{C}
, together with a splitting
s

there is a map

B_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,s}:HC^{*}(\mathcal{C})[-1]\rightarrow MCE\Big(Cyc^{*}_{+}(\mathcal{C})[-1],d_{cyc},\{\_,\_\}_{o}\Big),

(13.4.3)

mapping the zero vector to the zero Maurer-Cartan element. It follows similar to lemma
6.1.4
that (up to finiteness aspects) we can identify elements of the right hand side of map (
13.4.3
) with cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories, the zero Maurer-Cartan element corresponding to our initial category
\mathcal{C}
.
Thus the map
B_{\mathcal{C},\lambda,s}
produces a family of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-categories parametrized by cyclic cohomology, which at zero gives our initial category. We may have to reformulate conjecture
13.4.1
with respect to this family induced by (
13.4.3
). One major caveat is that some elements of this family may be possibly curved, which we do not know how to treat with our formalism. We hope to work on this in the future.

We finish this section by explaining the origin of the map
13.4.3
, which we use to obtain a family version of conjecture H. This map is connected to the cyclic variant of Kontsevich’s formality result
[
70
]
; see remark
13.4.11
.

For that recall from definition
5.2.2
the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L(\mathcal{C}),
depending on
\mathcal{C}
a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category. Further denote by

CC^{*}(\mathcal{C}):=\Big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]^{\vee}[-1],d_{hoch}+uB\Big),

the abelian
L_{\infty}
-algebra with the same underlying graded vector space as
L(\mathcal{C})
. Given a collection of object
\Lambda\subseteq\mathcal{C}
, since the full subcategory on
\Lambda
is a cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category, we have the dg shifted Lie algebra (see around definition
6.1.7
)

O(\Lambda):=\Big(Cyc^{*}_{+}(\Lambda)[-1],d_{cyc},\{\_,\_\}_{o}\Big).

Theorem 13.4.4
.

Given a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and
\Lambda\subseteq\mathcal{C}
then there exist a (non-trivial)
L_{\infty}
-morphism
F_{D^{oc}}
on the right of diagram
13.4.5
. Assuming further the existence of a splitting
s
there exists a (non-trivial)
L_{\infty}
-morphism
F_{s}
on the left of diagram
13.4.5
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CC^{*}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O(\Lambda).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{D^{oc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{s}}

(13.4.5)

If
\Lambda\cong\mathcal{C}
these maps are in fact quasi-isomorphisms.

Sketch of Proof.

Let us focus on the right part of diagram
13.4.5
first. Recall, eg. from proposition 6.1 of
[
71
]
, that we can identify an
L_{\infty}
-map

F_{D^{oc}}:L(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow O(\Lambda)

with a Maurer-Cartan element in the convolution dg Lie algebra (see
[
71
]
for details)

Hom\big(Sym(L(\mathcal{C})[1]),O(\Lambda)\big).

(13.4.6)

There is an obvious map of graded vector spaces

Sym(L(\mathcal{C})[1])^{\vee}\otimes O(\lambda)\rightarrow Hom(Sym(L(\mathcal{C})[1]),O(\Lambda))

(13.4.7)

On the left hand side of equation (
13.4.7
) we use that the tensor product of a dg algebra (given as the linear dual of the dg colagebra defining the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
L(\mathcal{C})
, recalling definition
4.2.4
) and a dg shifted Lie algebra is again dg shifted Lie algebra in a natural way. Further one can directly show that the map
13.4.7
is a map of dg Lie algebras.

Next we recall that given a smooth cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
and a collection of objects
\Lambda\subseteq\mathcal{C}
that by definition
5.2.2
(but ignoring the closed shifted Poisson bracket) we can define a dg-algebra

C(\mathcal{C}):=\Big({\mathcal{F}^{c}(\mathcal{C})}|_{\gamma=0},\cdot,d_{hoch}+uB+\{\rho(S^{c}_{g=0}),\_\}_{c},\Big).

and that there is a natural map (induced from including a vector space into its double dual)

C(\mathcal{C})\otimes O(\Lambda)\rightarrow Sym(L(\mathcal{C})[1])^{\vee}\otimes O(\lambda).

(13.4.8)

Thus if we can show that there is a (non-trivial) Maurer-Cartan element in the left hand side of map (
13.4.8
) it follows after applying the (injective) maps (
13.4.8
) and (
13.4.7
) that we find a non-zero element of the dg-Lie algebra (
13.4.6
), which is equivalent to providing the
L_{\infty}
-map from the right hand side of diagram (
13.4.5
).

For that let us denote by
D^{oc}
the genus zero, one unframed boundary with arbitrary boundary marked points and arbitrary interior framed marked points part of string vertices, compare (
13.1.9
). Further recall that we denoted by
S^{c}|_{g=0}
the genus zero part of the closed string vertices and by
D
the part of the open-closed string vertices corresponding to the disk with arbitrary many boundary marked points but no interior marked points. Then equation (
13.1.9
) implies that

\partial D^{oc}+\{D^{oc},S^{c}|_{g=0}\}_{c}+\frac{1}{2}\{D^{oc},D^{oc}\}_{o}+\{D^{oc},D\}_{o}=0

(13.4.9)

Since we have

d_{cyc}=d+\{\rho(D),\_\}_{o}

(compare the comment after corollary
13.1.11
) it follows from theorem
13.1.5
that

\rho_{\mathcal{C},\Lambda}(D^{oc})\in MCE(C(\mathcal{C})\otimes O(\Lambda)),

(13.4.10)

which is the Maurer-Cartan element that we asked for.

The fact that this Maurer-Cartan element is non-trivial should follow from the results of section 7.4 of
[
36
]
, which imply that the component of
\rho_{\Lambda,\Lambda}(D^{oc})
with just one interior marked point exactly induces (as the linear part of the
L_{\infty}
-morphism induced by (
13.4.10
)) the quasi-isomorphism

\big(CH_{*}(\Lambda)[u^{-1}]^{\vee}[-1],d_{hoch}+uB\big)\rightarrow\big(Cyc^{*}_{+}(\Lambda)[-1],d_{cyc}\big)

of two different models computing cyclic cohomology (see section 2.1.11 of
[
76
]
). We leave making this more precise to future work.

Assuming additionally the existence of a splitting
s
the loop zero part of the map from theorem
1.2.10
induces (as it is a map of algebras, which follows from the
\gamma=0
part of theorem
12.3.16
) an
L_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism

L(\mathcal{C})\rightsquigarrow\Big(\big(CH_{*}(\mathcal{C})[u^{-1}]\big)^{\vee},d_{hoch}+uB\Big),

which explains the left arrow in diagram
13.4.5
.
∎

Remark 13.4.11
.

The element
D^{oc}
(in fact only its one marked point part) plays, at least heuristically, a crucial part in Costello-Li’s analytic approach to quantizing open-closed BCOV theory
[
33
]
, which we introduced in section 3.1. They further note the relation to the cyclic formality version
[
95
]
of Kontsevich’s formality theorem
[
70
]
.

In another direction, the map (
13.4.3
) should be related to what is called bulk-boundary deformation in the symplectic context, compare
[
45
]
.
